EQUAL ACCESS TO JUSTICE ACT TAX DIVISION MANUAL: 

STATEMENT OF POLICIES AND GUIDELINES I. GENERAL OVERVIEW. 

A. Purpose. 

The Equal Access to Justice Act, Pub. L. 

No. 96-481, Title II, 94 Stat. 2321, 2325 

(the Act), enacted on October 21, 1980, effective October 1, 1981, authorizes the award of of attorney fees and other expenses against the United States in certain civil proceedings and under certain conditions. 

The Act is premised on the Congressional finding that certain individuals, businesses, and other organizations may be deterred from defending against "unreasonable" governmental civil action because of the expense involved. 

In Congress' view the greater resources and expertise of the United States demand that, in certain situations, the United States be liable for attorney fees, although a private litigant would not be liable. In addition, the Act also waives sovereign immunity provide for the applicability of, in actions by or against the United States, the common law and statutory exceptions to the "American rule" respecting the award of attorney fees. 1/

B. Scope. 

The Act is comprised of two principal sections. It provides for the award of fees and other expenses in certain "agency actions" and in certain "judicial proceedings." 

1/ Sec. 202 of the Act. 

- 1 -

1. Agency actions. 

Section 203 of the Act amends the Administrative Procedure Act, 5 U.S.C., by adding new Section 504 

Section 504 which provides that attorney fees may be awarded in agency adjudications conducted pursuant to 5 U.S.C., Section 554. Section 554(a) applies: 

[I]n every case of an adjudication required by statute to be determined on the record after an opportunity for an agency hearing, hearing, except to the extent that there is involved-- (1) a matter subject to a subsequent trial of the law and the facts de novo in a court; !!

Internal Revenue Service determinations of tax liability or actions to collect taxes are matters "subject to a subsequent trial of the law and the facts de novo in a court. 

Moreover, it has has been recognized that tax liability determinations are not subject to the requirements of the Administrative

Procedure Act. 2/ 

Advance ruling and technical advice proceedings are similarly excluded from coverage under the "agency actions" section of 

See Salman

V. 

V. 

Commissioner, 1948); Cohen V. (10th Cir. 1949); 2/ 

Swanson, 80-2 U.S.T.C., para. 9574 (Nev. May 30, 1980). Also see

Kennedy Name Plate Co. 

170 F. 2d 196 (9th Cir. 

Commissioner, 176 F. 2d 394 

O'Dwyer V. Commissioner, 

(4th Cir. 1959). 

266 F. 2d 575 

2 -

the Act.

Accordingly, the Act has no appli cability to Internal Revenue Service adminis tax determination and collection trative proceedings. 

2. Judicial proceedings. 

The Act's provisions regarding "judicial proceedings" are applicable to many civil proceedings handled by Tax Division attorneys in the federal court system. Under the 

American rule, each party is generally responsible for the payment of his or her own attorney fees and other expenses incurred during litigation. In the case of civil actions brought by or against the United 

States, 28 U.S.C., Section 2412, has provided since 1966 that costs, but not attorney fees, could be awarded against the United States, unless otherwise specifically provided by statute.

The Act makes two essential changes in the Section 2412 rule prohibiting awards of attorney fees against the United States except where mandated by statute: first, new

Section 2412(b) makes the United States liable for fees and expenses of attorneys to the same extent that any other party would be liable under the common law exceptions to the American rule and under statutes that provide for a fee award; and second, new Section 

2412(d) provides that in civil actions (other than tort actions) pending between October 1, 1981, and September 30, 1984, brought by or against the United States, federal courts shall award attorney fees, expert witness fees, and other described expenses of litigation to qualifying parties who prevail against the United States, unless the position of the United States as a party to the proceeding was substantially justified or special circumstances make an award unjust. • 3

Finally, the Act amends Rule 37 of the

Federal Rules of Civil Procedure by deleting subparagraph (f) which heretofore prohibited the award of expenses and fees against the 

United States under the rule.

C. Legislative history. 

The process which culminated in enactment of the Act began with hearings during during the 

93d, 94th, and 95th Congresses. 3/ In the 

95th Congress, the Senate passed an earlier version of the Act as part of the Legal 

Services Corporation Act Amendments of 1977 4/ 

but the amendment was deleted in conference.

In the 96th Congress, the Senate sponsors of the Act introduced the legislation as 

S. 265, 96th Cong., 1st Sess. (1979). After hearings, 5/ the bill cleared the Senate 

Judiciary Committee unanimously and passed the Senate on July 31, 1979, by a vote of 

94 to 3.

In the House, numerous similar bills were referred to the Judiciary Committee; of those bills, H.R. 6429, 96th Cong., 2d Sess. (1980), the Small Business Equal 

Access to Justice Act, was jointly referred to the Judiciary and Small Business Committees.

D. Legal context. 

The Act 14/ directs agencies to establish uniform procedures for the award of fees in their administrative proceedings, after consultation with the Chairman of the Administrative Conference of the United States (ACUS). 15/

ACUS has developed model regulations to assist agencies in establishing their own regulations pursuant to the requirements of the Act. Draft model regulations appeared in the Federal Register on March 10, 1981. 16/ 

13/ H.R. 3882, 97th Cong., 1st Sess. (1981). 

14/ Sec. 504(c)(1) of the Act. 

15/ ACUS was established as a permanent independent federal agency by the Administra tive Conference Act of 1964, 5 U.S.C., Sections 

571 through 576. Its purpose is to identify the causes of inefficiency, delay and unfairness in administrative proceedings affecting private rights, and to recommend improvements to the President, the agencies, the Congress, and the courts. 

16/ 46 Fed. Reg. 15895 (March 10, 1981). 

7 -

After a period for public comment the regulations were revised and issued in their final form. 17/ 

17/ During the public comment period, the Justice Department criticized the draft regulations by letter dated May 12, 1981, from Edward C. Schmults, Deputy Attorney

General. 

Since the ACUS model regulations apply only to administrative proceedings, they are largely irrelevant to the award of fees and other expenses under the Act in judicial proceedings. Many of the terms contained in the "agency action" portion, however, are identical to those in the "judicial proceedings" section of the Act. It is anticipated, therefore, that the ACUS model rules will be cited as interpretative authority in future judicial award proceedings under the Act. 

Since the ACUS

ACUS regulations do not reflect Department of Justice interpretations in a number of respects, the Tax Division will not cite those regulations as authority. 18/ 

The legal context for early interpretation of the Act also also includes many federal statutes authorizing the award of attorney fees. 19/ These statutes were enacted for many

Some of the case law will be relevant, however, especially where the statutes use language identical with the Act. The legislative history of the Act specifically notes that the definition of the term "prevailing party" used in the Act has been the subject of litigation under existing fee-shifting statutes and states that the interpretation of the term in the Act should be consistent with the law that has developed under existing statutes. 20/

II. 

SECTION 2412(b): 

AWARD OF ATTORNEY FEES AGAINST THE UNITED STATES ON THE SAME BASIS AS A PRIVATE PARTY. 

A. The "American rule" and its exceptions.

Under the American rule, the prevailing litigant is ordinarily not entitled to collect attorney fees from the loser. The 

Supreme Court in Alyeska Pipeline Service Co. 

V. Wilderness Society, 21/ strictly limited the equitable power of the federal courts to award attorney fees to certain historically recognized situations. Specifically, the

Court reaffirmed three equitable bases for such awards: the common fund, the common benefit, and the bad faith rationales. (footnote continued) 19/ 

Justice of the Committee on the Judiciary, House of Representatives, May 20 and 

June 24, 1980, 96th Cong., 2d Sess. at 

611-629 (1980). 

20/ S. Rep. No. 96-253, 96th Cong., 1st 

Sess. at 7; H.R. Rep. No. 96-1418, 96th 

Cong., 2d Sess. at 11. 

21/ 421 U.S. 240 (1975). 9 -

The common fund rationale is exemplified by the Supreme Court in Trustees V. 

Greenough, 22/ a a suit alleging, inter alia, that the trustees were wasting secured assets. 

The suit succeeded in bringing the assets within the control of the court. The Supreme 

Court held that the complainant was entitled to reimbursement for his cost, including his attorney fees, out of the fund itself on the principle of unjust enrichment. In Central 

R. R. & Banking Com. of Georgia v. Pettus, 23/ the Supreme Court extended the principle, holding in that case that the attorney had an independent claim to reimbursement from the fund.

The common benefit theory extends the rationale of the common fund principle to permit the award of attorney fees in a case where the attorney actually did not create a fund. 24/ In Mills V.

Electric Auto-Lite 

Co., 25/ the Supreme Court awarded attorney fees to the shareholders of Electric Auto-Lite who had brought a stockholder derivative action against the corporation, successfully establishing that its merger with another corporation had been tainted by misleading proxy statements. Although there was no monetary recovery, the Court considered it proper to charge the plaintiffs' attorney fees to the defendant corporation because the corporation was in fact the beneficiary of the lawsuit. 22/ 

105 U.S. 527 (1881). 23/ 

24/ Sprague v. Ticonic National Bank, 307 U.S. 161 (1939). 

25/ 396 U.S. 375 (1970). 

10 - 

---

The final equitable rationale for imposing a fee obligation that survives Alyeska is the bad faith doctrine. The courts may award attorney fees for "'willful disobedience of a court order . as part of the fine to be levied on the defendant' 26/ or "when the losing party has acted in bad faith, vexatiously, wantonly, or for oppressive reasons 

27/ 

Usually, bad faith is found in a party's dilatory or obstructionist conduct during the litigation itself. 28/ There is authority, however, for awarding fees based upon the bad faith of the party's conduct that gave rise to the litigation. 29/ 26/ 

Alyeska Pipeline Service Co. V. Wilder ness Society, supra, 421 U.S. at 258, quoting 

Fleischmann Distilling Corp. v. Maier Brewing

Co., 386 U.S. 714, 718 (1967). 

27/ Id. at 258-259, quoting F. D. Rich Co. 

Industrial Lumber Co., 417 U.S. 116, 129

V. 

28/ See, e.g.,

V. 

Red School House, Inc. 

ОЕО, 386 F. Supp. 1177, 1193-1194 (Minn. 

1974); Gates V. Collier, 371 F. Supp. 1368 (N.D. Miss. 1973), aff'd, 489 F. 2d 298 (5th Cir. 1973), vacated and remanded, Amos, 1972), aff'd, 522 F. 2d 81 (5th Cir. 1975); Sims v. 

340 F. Supp. 691 (M.D. Ala.

11

In the absence of a common fund, a common benefit, or bad faith, it is clear under Alyeska that the federal courts do not have the power to award attorney fees unless the authority has been specifically conferred upon them by by a statutory exception to 

American rule.

B. 

the 

The "American rule" and the award of attorney fees against the

United States. 

Section 2412, 28 U.S.C., specifically addresses the subject of the award of costs and fees against the United States or any agency and any official of the United States acting in his or her official capacity. 

Prior to its amendment by the Act, Section 

2412 expressly proscribed an award of attorney fees against the United States, even under the common law exceptions, in the absence of specific statutory authority. The new Sec tion 2412(b) will make the United States liable for attorney fees to the same extent that any other party would be liable under the common law exceptions to the American rule and under statutes that provide for fee awards (even under statutes that do not specifically authorize awards against 

United States).

Section 2412 (b) provides as follows: 

(b) Unless expressly prohibited by statute, a court may award reasonable fees and expenses of attorneys, in addition to the costs which may be awarded pursuant to subsection (a), to the prevailing party in any civil action brought by or against the United States any agency and any official of the 

United States acting in his or her official capacity in any court the 

12 

---

having jurisdiction of such action.

The United States shall be liable for such fees and expenses to the same extent that any other party would be liable under the common law or under the terms of any statute which specifically provides for such an award.

This subsection "reflects the belief that at a minimum, the United States should be held to the same standard in litigating as private parties and that no justification exists for exempting the United States from fee awards in these limited situations. 30/

C. !!

Fee exposure under Section 2412(b) in Tax Division cases. 

The Tax Division will contend that the only courts authorized to make awards under 

Section 2412(b) are those defined in 28 

U.S.C., Sec. 451 such as the district courts, the courts of appeals and the Court of Claims, but not the state courts, the bankruptcy courts, nor the Tax Court. 31/ Attorney fees and expenses incurred by a prevailing party in appealing a decision of the Tax Court may be subject to an award by the court of appeals since a court of appeals is a "court of the 

United States. 

11 

Any exposure to attorney fee awards under Section 2412(b) in cases handled by the Tax Division will emanate from the common law 

30 S. Rep. No. 96-253, supra at 19; see also, H. R. Rep. No. 96-1418, supra at 17. 

31/ H.R. Rep. No. 96-1418, supra at 17. 

13.

exceptions to the American rule as they may be applied by these courts. 32/

The bad faith exception may be frequently pleaded since, under the Allen Amendment, 33/ the only previous statutory provision allowing attorney fee awards in tax tax cases, the standards applied by the courts generally focused on whether the United States' position or behavior was frivolous, harassing, vexatious, unreasonable, or without foundation, or whether an action was instituted in bad faith.

The decisional law interpreting the 

Allen Amendment essentially uses the Chris tiansburg Garment Co.

of subjective bad faith. The common law bad faith exception, in contrast, has generally been interpreted to require subjective bad faith. 35/

The factual situation presented in re- 

Bryant V. United States 36/, although solved under the Allen Amendment, is an example of the kind of case where there may be exposure under the bad the bad faith exception. 

The case involved 100% 

100% penalty assessments made against Mrs. Bryant who was employed as a bookkeeper for a contractor which had a long history of making untimely withholding tax payments and a Mr. Borden, who controlled and supervised the company's operations.

Bryant had paid $10 of the assessment and sued for a refund. The Government then counterclaimed for the balance owed on the assessment, approximately $80,000. Although the court found that at the time of the assessment the Internal Revenue Service could reasonably have concluded that Bryant was liable under Code Section 6672, the court also found as a fact that the Government's purpose in filing the counterclaim counterclaim against her was not to collect the assessment but rather was a trial tactic to secure her testimony against Borden. It based its finding upon the sworn testimony of the plaintiff's attorneys, attorneys, rejecting the testimony of Government counsel which denied the trial tactics charge. The court concluded that the counterclaim was filed for the purpose of "intimidating" Bryant and was, therefore, instituted in bad faith. 35/

15

It is anticipated that the common fund and common benefit rationales will be raised in cases in interpleader or in the nature of interpleader. In those cases under prior law, attorney fees and costs were commonly claimed either by the attorney for a stakeholder or by an attorney who "created a fund" from which which competing creditors might claim payment. 

Except in rare instances, fees have been denied when payment of the fees from the interpleader fund would impair enforcement of a federal tax lien. 37/ The ground for this holding has been that if a court were to permit invasion of the fund for this reason, it would be improperly awarding to the party claiming attorney fees a priority of lien over that of the United States, despite the fact that the claimant's claim was "inchoate" when the federal tax lien attached. The superpriority for an attorney's lien provided by Section 6323(b)(8) of the Code is a statu tory exception to this principle. 

Most court cases which have confronted the issue of the source of the power of a federal court to award costs and attorney fees out of an interpleader fund have assumed that a federal court is possessed of those powers which characterized the traditional equity court. Other courts, however, have concluded that their power was dependent on whether a court of the forum state could award such fees.

In any event, we expect that both a party whose attorney created a fund and a party who acted as stakeholder and incurred attorney fees for this reason will argue that 

37/ See, e.g., United States 

Constr. Co., 355 U.S. 587 (1958).

they are entitled to reimbursement under the common fund and/or common benefit exceptions to the American rule. 38/

It is the Tax Division's view that Section 2412(b) is a waiver of sovereign immunity which will render the United States liable for such fees and expenses to the same extent that any other party would be liable under the common law or under the terms of a statute. We do not view Section 6323 of the 

Code as an express prohibition of such an award.

It should be kept in mind, however, that the common benefit and common fund exceptions are equitable exceptions and the award of fees and expenses is discretionary with the court. It will be necessary, therefore, to become thoroughly familiar with arguments used to reduce or deny such awards, for example, the self-interest of the claimant in the outcome of the law suit, in order to successfully defend against attorney fees in this previously well-settled area of the law.

on Section 208 of the Act provides that the amendments to the Act shall take effect 

October 1, 1981, and shall apply to any civil action described in 28 U.S.C., Section 2412, which is pending on, or commenced on or after, that date. The fact that a case may be pending, by itself, will not determine the eligibility of civil action for an award under Section 

2412(d). Section 2412 (d) (1) (B) of the Act also requires a party to file an application for an award "within thirty days of final judgment in the action." "! 

In district court cases, the Tax Division interprets the term "final judgment, used in Section 2412(d)(1)(B) as an noninterlocutory judgment entered by a district court. This interpretation is consistent with the meaning given to the term in Rule 

54(b) and (c) (relating to judgments), Rule 60(b) (relating to relief from a judgment or order), and Rule 62(a) and (c) (relating to stay of proceedings to enforce a judgment) of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. Thus, 

"final judgment means the same as "final decision" in 28 U.S.C., Section 1291 (relat ing to the jurisdiction of courts of appeals), and the 30 days for filing an application for an award begins to run from the entry of final judgment. Thus, if a timely motion for judgment notwithstanding the verdict (Rule 

50(b)), or motion to amend or make additional findings (Rule 52(b)), is made, grant or !! 

- 23

denial of such motion would start the 30-day period running, as would the denial of a motion for a new trial (Rule 59).

The Court of Claims has defined the term "final judgment" as used in Section 

2412(d)(1)(B) as "the entry by the court of a final judgment for the payment of money, or for the dismissal of a petition, or of a final order in an appeal under Chapter XV, or of a final judgment as defined in Rule 

147(d)." 53/

A judgment entered in a court of appeals under Rule 36 of the Federal Rules of Appellate Procedure, or a final order of the

Supreme Court will be considered a "final judgment" for 2412(d)(1)(B). purposes of 

Section

Supreme Court results in a remand, an application for an award may be filed within thirty days of the entry of final judgment on remand.

C. Eligible party 

To be eligible for an award of attorney fees and other expenses under the Act, an applicant must be a "party" to a civil action. 

The term "party" is defined in Section 

an indi- did [P]arty' means (i) vidual whose net worth not exceed $1,000,000 at the time the civil action was filed, (ii) a sole cwner of an unincorporated business, or a partnership, corporation, association, or organization whose net worth did not exceed $5,000,000 at the time the civil action was filed, except that an organization described in section 501(c)(3) of the Internal Revenue Code of 1954 (26 U.S.C. 501(c)(3)) exempt from taxation under section 501(a) of the Code and a cooperative association as defined in section 15(a) 

of the Agricultural Marketing Act 

(12 U.S.C. 1141j(a)), may be a party regardless of the net worth of such organization or cooperative association, or (iii) a sole owner of an unincorporated business, or a partnership, corporation, association, or organization, having not more than 500 employees at the time the civil action was filed* * * 54/ See Nibali v.

The agency adjudication segment of the Act also defines party, but uses a different definitional approach. The result is that the definition of "party" under Section 

2412(d) (2) (B) could be read more broadly than the definition contained in 5 U.S.C., Section 504(b)(1)(B), to treat any business with 500 or fewer employees, even if its net worth is more than $5 million, as as a "party" eligible for fees and expenses.

The effect of the definitions of "party" is to exclude from eligibility for an award certain categories of persons. Both definitions contain three exclusions, a $1 million net worth exclusion, a $5 million net worth exclusion and a 500 employee exclusion.

Under the definitions, an individual having a net worth of more than $1 million is not an eligible party; nor is a sole owner of a proprietorship, 55/ a partnership, associaorganization eligible if its net worth exceeds $5 million. Code Section 

501(c)(3) organizations and agricultural cooperatives, however, are not subject to a net worth limitation. 56/ tion, 55/ We will contend that an individual is subject to the $1 million limitation when suing in a personal capacity and to the $5 million limitation when suing in the capacity of sole proprietor. Both personal and business assets should be taken into account in calculating either net worth limitation. 

56/ We will contend that governmental bodies can not be qualifying parties based upon testimony of a Senate sponsor of the legislation indicating that governmental bodies were not intended to be covered by the Act.

Factors may exist surrounding the issue of eligibility which arguably could constitute special circumstances making an award unjust even though Section 2412(b)(2)(B), if strictly construed, might not be interpreted to render the party ineligible. For example, prevailing party may have disposed of assets or incurred obligations solely for the purpose of meeting an eligibility standard or may have more than a $5 million net worth or more than 500 

500 employees if the assets and employees of its affiliates are taken into account. In light of the legislative intent to limit the relief accorded by the Act to small businesses, it may be worthwhile to explore and fully develop the facts surrounding the matters of the prevailing party's net worth and number of employees in order to see whether or not a special circumstances defense is available.

Another threshold inquiry, is the taxpayer, or other party claiming award, is a "prevailing party. That term is not defined in the Act. The Committee Reports to S. 265 state Congress intended the term to be consistent with the law that has developed under existing statutes using that term and that decisional law is described in the Reports as follows: 62/

E. What is substantial justification. 

Section 2412(d) (1) (A) provides for the award of of attorney fees and other expenses "unless the court finds that the position of the United States was substantially justified or that special circumstances make an award unjust."

The standard is based on the language in Rule 37 of the Federal Rules of

Civil Procedure which prohibits the imposition of sanctions under the rule if the party's action "was substantially justified or other circumstances made an award unjust. 71/ 

68/ 

Grubbs v. Butz, Butz, 548 F. 2d 973 (D.C. Cir. 

1976); Hanrahan v. Hampton, supra; but see 

Van Hoomissen v. Xerox Corp., 503 F. 2d 1131, 1133 (9th Cir. 1974). 

69/ See Biberman V. FBI, 496 F. Supp. (S.D. N.Y. 1980). 

70/ No. 79-1984 (D.C. Cir. Oct. 2, 1980). 

263 71/ 

S. Rep. No. 96-253, supra at 20; H.R. 

Rep. No. 96-1418, supra at 18. 

34 -

The meaning of the standard as used in

Rule 37 is explained by the Advisory Com mittee of the Judicial Conference of the which drafted it as 

States 

United follows: 72/ 

On many occasions, to be sure, the dispute over discovery between the parties is genuine, though ultimately resolved one way or the other by the court. In such cases, the losing party is substantially justified in carrying the matter to court. But the rules should deter the abuse implicit in carrying or forcing a discovery dispute to court when no genuine dispute exists. 

And the potential or actual imposition of expenses is virtually the sole formal sanction in the rules to deter a party from pressing to a court hearing frivolous requests for or objections to discovery.

The Committee Reports to S. 265 expand on the meaning of the standard: 73/ 

The test of whether or not a 

Government action is substantially justified is essentially one of reasonableness. Where the Government can show that its case had a reasonable basis both in law and fact, no award will be made. 72/ 

48 F.R.D. 54; emphasis supplied. 

73/ H.R. Rep. No. 96-1418, supra at 10; 

S. Rep. No. 96-253, supra at 6. - 35

The United States has the burden of proving the reasonableness of its actions. 74/

Upon a showing of substantial justification, no award will be made despite the fact that the taxpayer or other party is the prevailing party.

The Committee Reports suggest that-- 75/ Certain types of case dispositions may indicate that the Government action was not substantially justified. A court should look closely at cases, for example, where there has been a judgment on the pleadings or where where there is is a directed verdict or where a prior suit on the same claim had had been dismissed. Such cases clearly raise the possibility that the 

Government was unreasonable pursuing the litigation. in 

The standard, however, should not be read to raise a presumption that the Government position was not substantially justified, simply because it lost the lost the case. Nor, in fact, does the standard require the Government to establish that its decision to litigate was based on a substantial probability of prevailing. Furthermore, the Government should not be held liable where special circumstances would make an award unjust. This safety value" helps to insure that the

Whether substantial justification exists will depend upon the facts of each case. A test of reasonableness in fact and in law is an objective test. The "reasonable basis in law and fact" standard raises the question of when it is to be applied. Generally, the first position to be tested is the position taken by the Tax Division in the litigation. 78/ Nevertheless, a position which appeared substantially justified at the commencement of a judicial proceeding may change its color. For example, in certain circumstances, perhaps because of the limitations of the administra tive summons procedures, the Internal Revenue 

Service might authorize the commencement of a suit before all the relevant facts are known.

The case may then require formal discovery before the United States' position in the matter can be properly evaluated. If upon receipt of sufficient information it appears unreasonable to continue this suit, an attempt should be made to conclude the litigation expeditiously.

Similarly, taxpayers on occasion have frustrated the Internal Revenue Service's efforts to correct their tax liabilities by refusing to provide records and otherwise being uncooperative. If such a taxpayer then supplies the necessary documentation after commencement of a refund suit with the result that the case is conceded, we should contend that our defense of the litigation, until such time as the substantiation was provided, was not unreasonable. As long as the litigation is is concluded within a reasonable time period, an award of fees and expenses should not be made in these circumstances. 

Vigorous enforcement of the tax laws will necessarily result in some cases in which 78/ An exception to this general statement probably exists where the Tax Division concedes a case before taking a position on the record. 39

taxpayers prevail. The Act's legitimate purpose of stemming unreasonable Government action, however, should not inhibit vigorous and reasonable administration of the tax laws by either the Tax Division or the Tax Division or the Internal 

Revenue Service. While the Act will require constant reevaluation of a case in light of new information gained, the Tax Division should not be prevented from raising and litigating any meritorious issue. 

F. Special circumstances which make an award unjust.

Even if the position of the United States in the civil action is found not to be substantially justified, special circumstances may exist which would render an award of fees and expenses unjust. 79/ The Committee

Reports to S. 265 describe the 'special circumstances" provision as a "safety valve" which-- 80/ [H]elps to insure that the Government is not deterred from advancing in good faith the novel but credible extensions and interpretations of the law that often underlie vigorous enforcement efforts. It also gives the Court discretion to deny awards where equitable considerations dictate an award should not be made. 

The "special circumstances" provision derives from Rule 37 of the Federal Rules of 

Civil Procedure, as does the "substantially 79/ 

Sec. 2412(d)(1)(A).

G. Denial for unreasonable delay of final resolution. 

A third ground for reduction or denial of an award of attorney fees to a prevailing party is found in Section 2412 (d)(1)(C) which provides that-- to be awarded 

The court, in its discretion, may reduce the amount pursuant to this subsection, or deny an award, to the extent that the prevailing party party during the course of the proceedings engaged in conduct which unduly and unreasonably protracted the final resolution of the matter in controversy. 

Unwarranted resistance to formal discovery and groundless motions may, in appropriate circumstances, constitute such conduct.

46

Section 206 of the Act provides that nothing in Section 2412(d) alters, modifies, repeals, invalidates, or supersedes any other provision of federal law which authorizes an award of attorney fees and other expenses to any party other than the United States that prevails in any civil action brought by or against the United States. Section 206 of the Act is a limitation on the civil actions to which Section 2412(d) may be applicable.

This interpretation is in accord with the legislative history which states: 100/ [S]ubsection [2412(d) (1)] applies to all civil actions except those sounding in tort and those already covered by existing fee-shifting statutes.

Accordingly, we will urge that an applicant is not entitled to an award under Section 2412(d) where the case is covered by another fee shifting provision, such as by the Freedom of Information Act attorney fee provision. 

Such claims are anticipated, however, since even though the Freedom of Information Act has the advantage of no limitation on the hourly rate on attorney fees such as the one established by Section 2412(d)(2)(A), it does not allow for payment of the broad range of expenses described in Section 2412(d)(2)(A). 

I. Fees and expenses allowable. 

The "fees and other expenses" allowable under Section 2412(d) are those "incurred by that party in any civil action (other than cases sounding in tort) brought by or against 

100/ S. Rep. No. 96-253, supra at 20. - 47

the United States in any court having jurisdiction of that action... 101/ The Tax

Division's position is that fees and expenses incurred during the Internal Revenue Service administrative process may not be awarded because they would not have been incurred in a civil action. 

Fees and expenses awardable in civil actions include the reasonable expenses of expert witnesses, costs of any study, analysis, engineering report, test, or project found by the court "to be necessary for the preparation of the party's case" and "reasonable" attorney fees. The amount of fees awarded are to be based on "prevailing market rates" for the kind and quality of services furnished, except that expert witnesses are not to be compensated at a rate in excess of the highest rate of compensation for experts paid by the United States. Also, attorney fees are not to be awarded in excess of $75 per hour, unless the court determines that an increase in the cost of living or a special factor, such such as the limited availability of qualified attorneys for the proceedings involved, justifies a higher fee. 102/ 

With regard to the ceiling on attorney fees, the Committee Reports state: 103/

J. Requirements of the application under Section 2412(d). 

An application for fees and expenses pursuant to Section 2412 (d) must be filed which "shows" that the party is a prevailing party and is eligible to receive an award.

The Act does not specify the precise form of the application. Presumably, the application in a judicial proceeding would be labelled "motion, "petition, or even "application, consistent with practice under existing fee-shifting statutes such as the Freedom of 

Information Act. 

!!

We interpret the statutory requirement that a party "show" it has prevailed to mandate an explanation by the applicant of the specific position of the United States as to which it prevailed. 

The statutory directive that the applicant "show" that it is eligible to receive an award likewise requires more than mere conclusory allegations of eligibility. The facts demonstrating the eligibility of a party for attorney fees under the statutory definition of "party, such as the net worth of the party and the number of its employees, usually will not be a matter of record prior to the filing of the application for an award. K. Response to application

The Act does not establish a time within which the United States must States must respond to an application for an award. Rule 7(b)(1) of the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure provides that "[a]n application to the court for an order shall be by motion." Accordingly, even if the application is application is not designated as a "motion, "! it should be treated as one for the purpose of establishing a time limit for response. Where there is no specific local rule establishing time limits under the Act, our response should be governed by the time limits set by local rules for responding to motions.

The Tax Division has agreed with the 

Office of Chief Counsel that unless there is an obvious need to address a particular issue under the Act when the initial defense or suit letter is prepared, the Office of Chief

Counsel will not discuss the attorney fee issue until its views are requested. 112/

The Tax Division generally will request the views of the Office of Chief Counsel as soon after the case has been decided, settled, or conceded as it appears probable to the Tax Division attorney that an application will be filed, or within two days after receiving notice that an application has been filed. 

The request should be sent to the office handling the case with a copy to the National 

Office of the Chief Counsel in cases handled by the District Counsel. Counsel will have 

20 days within which to respond. of Chief 112/ An exception will be made in that the 

Office 

Counsel will address the matter of a fee and expense award in in any letter by which it recommends full concession of a case. 

53

In cases where a likelihood there is that an award will be made, every effort should be made to await the recommendation of the Internal Revenue Service before filing a response. No concession of an attorney fee issue should be made without first receiving the views of the Internal Revenue Service. 

The response should raise any failure by an applicant to adequately support by affidavit an application. The response also should raise available defenses, such as substantial justification and special circumstances, and when facts necessary to oppose an application are already contained in the record of the case, the response should tie these into the discussion. It will be necessary in some instances, however, to obtain additional facts by discovery, informal or formal, or from the Internal Revenue Service. Where an application is made during a period when the Government is appealing or contemplating whether or not to appeal a loss, it it may be appropriate in some cases, such as those requiring substantial discovery or where the likelihood of success on appeal is great, to move for a stay of the award proceedings pending appeal.

L. Discovery 

Award proceedings will present will present factual issues upon which the parties may desire formal discovery: the United States may want discovery on the applicant's eligibility for an award, to verify the amount of fees and expenses being claimed or even to obtain admissions from the applicant which will support a claim of substantial justification or special circumstances; the applicant may attempt to discover the motives and reasons for the position of the United States which is allegedly substantially unjustified. 

54 

---

The Tax 

Division generally will not undertake and will oppose discovery on any factual issue solely relating to an award under Section 2412(d) until the court has rendered a decision on the merits of the litigation or the case has been conceded or settled while reserving the attorney fee issue. Until that time, the primary requirement for an award, i.e., that the applicant be a "prevailing party, generally will not exist. Premature discovery is wasteful of the parties' time and resources. Accordingly, we should respond to premature interrogatories, requests for production, and other discovery on these issues by making appropriate objections and/or by and/or by filing a motion for a protective order pursuant to 

Rule 26(c) requesting that discovery not be had until after the merits are determined. 

In the past, the Tax Division generally has been successful in protecting the opinions, conclusions, and reasoning of Internal Revenue 

Service personnel from discovery. 113/

Although the relevance of the opinions, conclusions, and reasoning of Internal Revenue 

Service and Department employees to the issues of substantial justification and special circumstances which would make an award unjust must be determined a caseby-case basis, the effort should continue to protect opinions, conclusions, and reasoning from disclosure. 

113/ See, e.g., ISI Corp. v. United States, 73-1 U.S.T.C., para. 9251 (N.D. Cal. Dec. 8, 

1972), aff'd, 503 F. 2d 558 (9th Cir. 1974); 

Weir Foundation v. United States, 362 F. Supp. 

928 (S.D. N.Y. 1973), aff'd per curiam, 508 F. 2d 894 (2d Cir. 1974); Simons-Eastern

Co. v. United States, 55 F.R.D. 88 (N.D. Ga. 

1972). 

55 -

The test for substantial justification, 11 i.e., "reasonable in law and fact, is an objective test. Thus, reasons why legal positions which proved to be unsuccessful were taken, such as recommendation by the 

Internal Revenue Service 114/ or by a Tax 

Division supervisor, should generally be irrelevant under Section 2412(d) as well as the good faith of the attorney advancing the position, or even the institutional good faith of the Department. 

If the facts known to the Internal

Revenue Service becomes a relevant issue, the Tax Division may find it necessary to develop additional facts beyond those contained in the record or the administrative file. For example, the administrative file may contain a copy of a summons for taxpayer records and a mere notation in the history sheets that the records were not produced. While making the summons a part of the record will be helpful, an affidavit from the agent explaining the full circumstances surrounding the failure to produce may buttress the position.

The Office of Chief Counsel has instructed its attorneys to assist Tax Division attorneys in locating revenue agents and officers, in marshaling evidence from them, and assisting in the the preparation of affidavits for for their signatures. 

Discovery on the eligibility of a prevailing party for an award under Section 

2412(d) may be required, especially if the courts are not strict in requiring the details 114/ An exception may be where the United States fully concedes a case without filing a response in opposition. The position of the 

Internal Revenue Service which may have caused the law suit may be the proper position to test in such a case.

of a party's net worth to accompany the application. Informal discovery, i.e., asking opposing counsel to supply the information by affidavit, should ordinarily be the first step. If that is not successful, interrogatories, requests for the production of documents and, possibly, depositions should be used.

Verification of net worth statements on a wholesale basis would not be cost beneficial. Thus, detailed sworn or verified net worth statements will be accepted at face value, unless there is reason for suspicion.

While the Internal Revenue Service will perform supplemental investigations on net worth when necessary, we will not routinely request them. The Internal Revenue Service, however, should be requested to forward a party's tax return for the year within which suit was instituted in sufficient time to afford the Internal Revenue Service the opportunity to locate and forward it. The last of these issues, mining the reasonableness of the fees and expenses claimed will usually be extremely time consuming. Every effort should be made to have the court defer this issue until the other issues establishing entitlement have been resolved.

730, 116/ an evidentiary hearing is appropriate. 117/ A number of lower courts require an evidentiary hearing whenever facts pertaining to a fee award are in dispute. 118/ When a court decided a fee question revolving around the the calculation of the award after witnessing the conduct of the entire case and with substantial briefs from the parties, however, the failure to hold a hearing was held to be not in error, especially when the

fees and expenses allowable include the reasonable expenses of expert witnesses, the reasonable cost of any study, analysis, engineering report, test, or project which is found by the court to be necessary for the preparation of the party's case case and reasonable attorney fees. 123/ The amount of fees awarded will be based upon prevailing market rates for the kind and quality of the services furnished, except that (i) no expert witness will be compensated at a rate in excess of the highest rate of compensation for expert witnesses paid by the United States; and (ii) attorney fees will not be awarded in excess of $75 per hour unless the court determines that an increase in the cost of living or a special factor, such as the limited availability of qualified attorneys for the proceedings involved, justifies a higher fee. 

In cases handled by the Tax Division, the potential for an award of fees and expenses exists only for for work performed in judicial proceedings. Fees and expenses incurred in the Internal Revenue Service administrative process are not recoverable.

Since fees and expenses of Internal Revenue 

Service administrative proceedings are not compensable under the Act, we will contend that the cost of a study, analysis, or expert's to

report which was presented during the administrative proceeding and again during the trial should not be compensable.

There is a substantial body of law under other fee-shifting statutes on the calculation of attorney fees. Any fee-setting inquiry, must produce an award which is in accord with the primary purpose of the statute. 124/ For example, under Title II of the Civil Rights Act of 1964, the purpose of awarding fees is "to encourage individuals injured by * * * discrimination to seek judicial relief." 125/ The purpose for awarding fees under the the Act, on the other hand, is "to diminish the deterrent effect" on private parties of seeking review of or defending against certain governmental action "because of the expense involved." 126/

It is an important obligation of Government counsel to protect the Government against excessive fee awards. The proper method for computing the amount of attorney fee awards is a recent and developing area of the law in which the courts are still searching for appropriate standards. 127/ The standards will ultimately depend upon depend upon the decisional law applicable to the case, i.e., the authority of the circuit. 124/ 

Copeland v. Marshall, 641 F. 2d 880 

(D.C. Cir. 1980). 

125/ Newman v. Piggie Park Enterprises,

Inc., 390 U.S. 400, 402 (1968). 

126/ 

Section 202 of the Act. 

127/ Copeland v. Marshall, supra at 909 (dissenting opinion). 61

Many courts have relied upon the Fifth 

Circuit opinion, Johnson v. Georgia Highway

Express, Inc. 128/, where the court suggested 

12 factors which should be considered by district courts for computing the amount of the fee in a Title VII case. These standards were drawn from guidelines recommended by the 

American Bar Association's Code of Professional Responsibility, Ethical Consideration 2-18 and Disciplinary Rule 2-106. The 12 

factors are as follows: 129/ (1) the time and labor required; (2) the novelty and difficulty of the question; (3) the skill requisite to perform the legal services properly; (4) the preclusion of other employment; (5) the customary fee in the community for similar work; (6) the fixed or contingent nature of the fee; (7) time limitations imposed by the client or the circumstances; (8) the amount involved and the results obtained; (9) the experience, reputation, and ability of the attorney; (10) the undesirability of the case; (11) (11) the nature and length of the professional relationship with the client; and (12) awards in similar cases. 

Although the Johnson factors are noteworthy, they are difficult to apply, largely because they are imprecise and overlap considerably. 130/ The en banc opinion of the 

District of Columbia Circuit in the Copeland case is helpful in analyzing the relationships among the factors and applying them to a factual setting although the authority of the circuit in which the case is pending must be examined to avoid taking a position which 

128/ 488 F. 2d 714 (5th Cir. 1974). 

129/ Id. at 717-719. 130/ 

Copeland V. 

Marshall, supra at 890. 62. 

---

has been rejected by that circuit or constitutes an unnecessary concession. 

Copeland adopts the view taken earlier by the Third Circuit in Lindy Bros. Builders,

Inc. v. American Radiator & Standard Sanitary 

Corp. 131/, that an attorney fee calculation begins with a court determining the "lodestar, that is, the number of "hours reasonably expended" multiplied by a "reasonable hourly rate."

Adjustments to this figure are appropriate, but the lodestar provides "'the only reasonably objective' starting point for awarding a fee." 132/ The following discussion seeks to adapt the Copeland approach to the Act. 

Hours reasonably expended.

An attorney should only be compensated for productive time which would ordinarily be properly billed to an attorney's client in the exercise of billing judgment. Under

Copeland, a party seeking an attorney fee must document document the number of hours of work performed. The Act, itself, requires an "itemized statement" from any attorney representing or appearing for a party stating the actual time expended and the rate at which fees were computed. 133/

There is no requirement in the Act that the itemized statement be Sworn to or verified under penalty of perjury. It is anticipated, however, that many applications for awards will be accompanied by the itemized statements of any attorneys and expert witness 

131/ 487 F. 2d 161 (3d Cir. 1973). 

132/ Copeland v. Marshall, supra at 891. 

133/ Sec. 2412(d)(1)(B). 

63

in affidavit form. In addition, some local rules may require that the itemized statement be sworn to or verified. 134/ In cases where itemized statement is not in affidavit form the attorney should be requested follow it up with a proper affidavit. 135/ to 

The affidavit should break down the number of hours per date expended expended by each attorney and the specific task during that time period.

The should include all of the actual performed breakdown hours of work performed, whether whether or not the attorney seeks compensation for those hours.

Attorney work sheets, time logs, and other timekeeping records may substitute for an affidavit or provide verification where necessary. If there is any reason to disbelieve an attorney's breakdown affidavit, or if the affidavit is not sufficiently detailed, such underlying documentation should be requested. For example, it will be necessary to see the underlying documentation if an attorney has questionable hours billed (such as a large number of hours billed for a brief that very closely resembles one previously filed, or billing of a large number of hours for a relatively short uncomplicated hearing), or has some inadequately described work. If any of these concerns cannot be explained satisfactorily a deposition may be required and/or the court may be requested to schedule a hearing on the number number of hours expended. 

134/ See, See, e.g., Rule 148(a) of the Rules of the United States Court of Claims, Appendix H, infra. 

135/ The same rules should apply to the itemized statement of an expert witness. 

64 - 

---

No compensation is due for nonproductive of time. For example, where three attorneys are present at a hearing when one would suffice, compensation should be denied for the excess time. Moreover, all attorney hours must be reviewed to evaluate the reasonableness the hours expended on particular projects. 

For instance, an expert tax attorney should not bill for the large blocks of research time on tax law to oppose a motion for summary judgment which a nonexpert attorney would. Where attorneys bill in units of time, an appropriate number of the units should be deducted if none of the attorney's units do not go below the minimum unit, regardless of the task. 

Attorneys should not be compensated for litigating claims upon which which they did not prevail. 136/ If the taxpayer prevails on a net operating loss claim and an investment credit claim but not on a travel and entertainment expense claim, hours that are spent on the travel and entertainment expense claim are not compensable.

The reason we, and the courts, need to know the actual number of hours expended in the detail set forth is so that we, and again the courts, can determine the reasonable number of hours expended. Billing judgment must be exercised by a party's counsel. 

Hours that an attorney ordinarily would not bill to a client that the attorney wanted to remain a client after receiving the bill should not be billed to the Government. If an attorney has exercised billing judgment, the attorney probably will not be seeking payment for every hour expended on a suit. 

136/ Nadeau V. Helgemoe, 581 F. 2d 275 (1st Cir. 1978); Dillon V. AFBIC Devel. 

Corp., 597 F. 2d 556 (5th Cir. 1979). 65

It will be necessary to calculate the total reasonable number of hours by each attorney for each type of work work performed.

The example used in Copeland was a table by level of experience of counsel, number of hours, and types of work such as court appearances, review of pleadings, research and drafting, and depositions.

While the Tax Division does not expect the courts to go over each attorney's affidavit or time sheets with a fine-toothed comb to determine whether all of the hours are reasonable, trial attorneys are are expected to go over each attorney's hours to determine whether the attorney is exercising the billing judgment that Copeland says the Government has a right to expect. 2. 

Reasonable hourly rate

The hourly rate at which an attorney may be compensated is limited by the Act to $75 per hour "unless the court determines that an increase in the cost of living or a special factor, such as the limited availability of qualified attorneys for the proceedings involved, justifies a higher fee." 137/ that

The Act defines "reasonable hourly rate" as the "prevailing market rates for the kind and quality of the services furnished. 138/

This definition is almost identical to adopted by the Copeland court. 139/ sonable hourly rate is the product of multiplicity of factors: 

the level of skill rea- 

138/ Sec. 2412(d) (2) (A). 

139/ Copeland v. Marshall, supra, 641 F. 2d at 892. 

66 

---

necessary, time limitations, the amount to be obtained in the litigation, the attorney's reputation and the undesirability of the case. 140/ There may be more than one reasonable hourly rate for each of the attorneys and for each of the kinds of work involved in the litigation. 

The truest test of the market rate for an attorney would, by definition, have to be that rate determined in the market or that amount that the attorney could get clients to pay out of their own pockets for work done by the attorney. Thus, the Tax Division will assert that a plaintiff claiming entitlement to a particular hourly rate must demonstrate that the majority of the attorney's paying clients in the market pay that attorney at that rate. A billing rate that is not paid by a client does not reflect the market rate.

It may not be possible for some attorneys to establish their Own market rate because they take most, if not all, of their cases on a contingent, or partially contingent, basis.

Attorneys in this position should be required come forward with affidavits from other attorneys of similar skill and experience in the community showing the fees charged by and paid to them. to 3. 

Adjustments to the lodestar.

The lodestar may be adjusted upward or downward by various factors. The burden is on the party proposing the deviation to justify it.

An adjustment to the lodestar may may be appropriate to compensate for the risk that the lawsuit may be unsuccessful and that no fee 

140/ Ibid. - 67 ·

may be awarded.

The contingency adjustment is a percentage increase of the lodestar; not a percentage of the amount of the of the recovery.

No contingency adjustment is appropriate where the hourly rate includes an allowance for the contingent nature of fees actions. 

Thus, if a party seeks a contingency adjustment to the lodestar, we should inquire, or request the court to inquire, inquire, into the elements of calculation of the attorney's hourly rate. If the hourly rate is at its level, in part, to compensate the attorney for the cases the attorney loses and does not receive fee, then no contingency adjustment is appropriate. A long delay in payment adjustment also may be incorporated into a contingency adjustment to allow for the fact that a market rate usually reflects clients who pay their bills. promptly. If the attorney uses the present hourly market rate of fees for work which was done long in the past when there was a lower rate or if the client paid the attorney all of the fee as the suit progressed, no adjustment would be proper. It follows that any attorney seeking this adjustment must be prepared to demonstrate that the attorney was not paid in the past and what the attorney's past hourly rates were.

The lodestar may be adjusted for the quality of representation only where it was unusually good or bad, taking into account the level of skill expected of an attorney charging the hourly rate used to compute the lodestar. The lodestar generally compensates attorneys adequately for their time. An adjustment should not be based upon the award given the client but upon the relationship of the award to what was to be expected. An upward adjustment would only be appropriate for exceptional results or performance. The 

Tax Division will not seek an adjustment on 

68 

---

the ground that an attorney's quality of representation was unusually poor without the prior authorization of the appropriate Sec tion Chief. 

Under other attorney fee statutes, courts which have considered the question have recognized the right to recover attorney fees for the time spent litigating the fee issue itself. 141/

A final point to remember is that the Act states that the fee is payable to the prevailing party, and not the attorney. 142/ 0. 

Considerations in conducting settlement negotiations.

The issue of fees and expenses is one which may be the subject of an offer in compromise, either as part of a settlement of the merits of a case, or by itself when raised after final judgment.

The legislative history for the Act indicates that a party may be deemed prevailing if the party obtains a favorable settlement of the case. 143/ Tax Division attorneys are encouraged to obtain a waiver of the award issue in any settlement of the merits.

It is the Tax Division's position that normally a compromise should should not be effected without resolution of the fee and expense issue: a contrary approach would mean that the 141/ 

L. Berger, Court Awarded Attorneys' 

Fees, What is "Reasonable"? 126 U. Pa. L. 

Rev. 281, 320 n.159 (1977). 

142/ See, Hamilton v. Ford Motor Company, 636 F.2d 745, (D.C. Cir. 1980). 

143/. H.R. Rep. No. 96-1418, supra at 11. 

69

same issue or issues theoretically compromised might have to be substantially litigated in order to determine whether attorney fees were due.

In a school desegregation class action, the Ninth Circuit questioned the ethical propriety of simultaneous negotiation of attorney fees and substantive issues in class action settlement discussions although it upheld the lower court's approval of the settlement involved nonetheless. 144/ The

First Circuit, however, in White v. New Hampshire Department of Employment Security, 145/ stated that it saw nothing wrong in a class action consent decree context with "requiring the parties to face up to the issue of fees in their settlement negotiations."

Insofar as an attorney fee claim may be waived, the Ninth Circuit's concern that an attorney's eagerness for a fee award would cause a conflict with the client's interest would be clearly unjustified. Moreover, the concerns expressed in class actions such as Mendoza and Prandini I, are not apposite to the tax cases normally handled by the Tax 

Division.

stated that an advantage to attorneys of making it clear in fee legislation that fees could be awarded for settlements, is that attorneys could then include the matter of the Tax Division settlement discusattorney fees in their settlement negotiations. 146/ Accordingly, will continue to combine sions of fee awards with discussions on the merits in its effort to obtain reasonable compromises which will save both the parties and the courts the time and expense of further litigation.

In cases where a waiver of attorney fees cannot be obtained, the fees and expenses to be conceded as part of an offer in compromise will be part of the amount conceded for the purpose of applying Tax Division Directive

The Internal Revenue Service will con tinue its present system of classifying some cases under the Settlement Option Procedures (SOP). An attorney fee issue under the Act will not be subject to the Settlement Option Procedures, however. Thus, the Tax Division will seek the recommendation of the Internal Revenue Service on any offer in compromise which, if accepted, would require the payment of fees and expenses under the Act. 148/ 

In light of the Act, the Tax Division has changed its practice of terminating full concession refund cases by a stipulation for dismissal with prejudice to, instead, permitting a judgment to be entered against the United States by means of a stipulation for entry of judgment. Leaving the award issue open in full concession cases is appropriate since ordinarily a decision to fully concede a case will not be affected by whether fees and expenses may be awarded. A stipulation for entry of judgment may be filed upon receipt from taxpayer's counsel; whereas it has been the practice to await receipt of the refund check before filing a stipulation for dismissal. Concession by judgment, therefore, should bring the attorney fee issue out in the open while the case is still fresh since the Act provides that a party seeking an award of fees and other expenses must submit an application to the court within 

30 days of final judgment in the action. 149/ 

148/ In full concession cases, ordinarily the Internal Revenue Service will provide us with their views concerning the allowability of attorney fees at the same time they recommend the concession of a case. 

149/ Sec. 2412(d)(1)(B).

It is important to remember that while a full concession may establish that the taxpayer is the prevailing party, it does not establish that the that the taxpayer is an eligible party or that the United States' position was not substantially justified. 

P. Relation to and effect on other fee award statutes.

Section 206 of the Act renders Section 

2412(d) inapplicable to all proceedings, such as FOIA and Privacy Act proceedings, covered by any other provision of federal law which authorizes an award of fees and other expenses against the United States.

Q. Payment of Section 2412(d) awards. 

Judgments in tax cases are paid out of a special appropriation for that purpose by the 

Commissioner of Internal Revenue. On the other hand, costs pursuant to Section 2412, now the new Section 2412 (a), are paid by the Comptroller General from the general judgment fund established by Section 1302, Supplemental Appropriation Act, 1957 Ch. 748, 70 Stat. 

678. 150/

Under the Act, an award under Section 

2412(b) will be paid from the general judgment fund unless the basis for the award is a finding that the United States acted in bad faith, then the award shall be paid by the agency itself. 151/ 

An award under Section 2412(d) will be payable "by any agency over which the party prevails." The question of whether the Justice

PART B-SMALL BUSINESS EXPORT FINANCING Small Business Export SEC. 110. This part may be cited as the "Small Business Export Expansion Act of Expansion Act of 1980". 

SEC. 111. (a) The Congress finds and declares that- 

(1) a strong export policy is essential to the health and wellbeing of the United States economy; (2) exports of goods and services account for one out of every six jobs in the manufacturing sector and 10 per centum of the gross national product. (3) every billion dollars in new exports is estimated to provide forty thousand jobs; (4) there is increased and fierce competition in international markets to United States goods and services; (5) small businesses account for no more than 10 per centum of all United States export sales; (6) Federal Government programs are not sufficently responsive to the needs of small business for export education and development of overseas marketing opportunities necessary to insure that small businesses realize their potential; and (7) it is in the national interest to systematically and consistently promote and encourage small business participation in international markets. (b) It is therefore the purpose of this part to encourage and promote small business exporting by- (1) providing educational and marketing assistance to small businesses; (2) insuring better access to export information and assistance for small businesses by upgrading and expanding the export development programs and services of the Department of Commerce and the Small Business Administration; and (3) promoting the competitive viability of such firms in export trade and encouraging increased tourism in the United States by creating a program to provide limited financial, technical, and management assistance as may be necessary. 

SEC. 112. Section 7(a) of the Small Business Act is amended by inserting before the phrase "The foregoing powers shall be subject, however,' the following new sentences: "The Administration is further empowered, only to such extent and in such amounts as are provided in appropriation Acts, to make or effect eithe: directly or in cooperation with banks or other lending institutions through agreements to participate on an immediate or deferred basis extensions and revolving lines of credit for export purposes to enable small business concerns to develop foreign markets and for preexport financing: Provided, however, That no such extension or revolving line of credit may be made for a period or periods exceeding eighteen months. A bank or participating lending institution may establish the rate of interest on extensions and revolving lines of credit as may be legal and reasonable."

PUBLIC LAW 96-481–OCT. 21, 1980 

94 STAT. 2325

TITLE II-EQUAL ACCESS TO JUSTICE ACT Equal Access to Justice Act. 

SEC. 201. This title may be cited as the "Equal Access to Justice 5 USC 504 note. 

Act".

FINDINGS AND PURPOSE 

SEC. 202. (a) The Congress finds that certain individuals, partner- 5 USC 504 note. ships, corporations, and labor and other organizations may be deterred from seeking review of, or defending against, unreasonable governmental action because of the expense involved in securing the vindication of their rights in civil actions and in administrative proceedings. (b) The Congress further finds that because of the greater resources and expertise of the United States the standard for an award of fees against the United States should be different from the standard governing an award against a private litigant, in certain situations. (c) It is the purpose of this title— (1) to diminish the deterrent effect of seeking review of, or defending against, governmental action by providing in specified situations an award of attorney fees, expert witness fees, and other costs against the United States; and (2) to insure the applicability in actions by or against the United States of the common law and statutory exceptions to the "American rule" respecting the award of attorney fees.

AWARD OF FEES AND OTHER EXPENSES IN CERTAIN AGENCY ACTIONS SEC. 203. (a)(1) Subchapter I of chapter 5 of title 5, United States 

Code, is amended by adding at the end thereof the following new section: 

"§ 504. Costs and fees of parties "(a)(1) An agency that conducts an adversary adjudication shall award, to a prevailing party other than the United States, fees and other expenses incurred by that party in connection with that proceeding, unless the adjudicative officer of the agency finds that the position of the agency as a party to the proceeding was substantially justified or that special circumstances make an award unjust. "(2) A party seeking an award of fees and other expenses shall, within thirty days of a final disposition in the adversary adjudication, submit to the agency an application which shows that the party is a prevailing party and is eligible to receive an award under this section, and the amount sought, including an itemized statement from any attorney, agent, or expert witness representing or appearing in behalf of the party stating the actual time expended and the rate at which fees and other expenses were computed. The party shall also allege that the position of the agency was not substantially justified. "(3) The adjudicative officer of the agency may reduce the amount to be awarded, or deny an award, to the extent that the party during the course of the proceedings engaged in conduct which unduly and unreasonably protracted the final resolution of the matter in controversy. The decision of the adjudicative officer of the agency under this section shall be made a part of the record containing the final decision of the agency and shall include written findings and conclusions and the reason or basis therefor. "(b)(1) For the purposes of this section-

"(A) 'fees and other expenses' includes the reasonable expenses of expert witnesses, the reasonable cost of any study, analysis, engineering report, test, or project which is found by the agency to be necessary for the preparation of the party's case, and reasonable attorney or agent fees (The amount of fees awarded under this section shall be based upon prevailing market rates for the kind and quality of the services furnished, except that (i) no expert witness shall be compensated at a rate in excess of the highest rate of compensation for expert witnesses paid by the agency involved, and (ii) attorney or agent fees shall not be awarded in excess of $75 per hour unless the agency determines by regulation that an increase in the cost of living or a special factor, such as the limited availability of qualified attorneys or agents for the proceedings involved, justifies a higher fee.); "(B) 'party' means a party, as defined in section 551(3) of this title, which is an individual, partnership, corporation, association, or public or private organization other than an agency, but excludes (i) any individual whose net worth exceeded $1,000,000 at the time the adversary adjudication was initiated, and any sole owner of an unincorporated business, or any partnership, corporation, association, or organization whose net worth exceeded $5,000,000 at the time the adversary adjudication was initiated, except that an organization described in section 

501(c)(3) of the Internal Revenue Code of 1954 (26 U.S.C. 501(c)(3)) 

exempt from taxation under section 501(a) of the Code and a cooperative association as defined in section 15(a) of the Agricultural Marketing Act (12 U.S.C. 1141j(a)), may be a party regardless of the net worth of such organization or cooperative association, and (ii) any sole owner of an unincorporated business, or any partnership, corporation, association, or organization, having more than 500 employees at the time the adversary adjudication was initiated; "(C) 'adversary adjudication' means an adjudication under section 554 of this title in which the position of the United States is represented by counsel or otherwise, but excludes an adjudication for the purpose of establishing or fixing a rate or for the purpose of granting or renewing a license; and "(D) 'adjudicative officer' means the deciding official, without regard to whether the official is designated as an administrative law judge, a hearing officer or examiner, or otherwise, who presided at the adversary adjudication. "(2) Except as otherwise provided in paragraph (1), the definitions provided in section 551 of this title apply to this section. 

"(c)(1) After consultation with the Chairman of the Administrative 

Conference of the United States, each agency shall by rule establish uniform procedures for the submission and consideration of applications for an award of fees and other expenses. If a court reviews the underlying decision of the adversary adjudication, an award for fees and other expenses may be made only pursuant to section 2412(d)(3) 

of title 28, United States Code. 

"(2) A party dissatisfed with the fee determination made under subsection (a) may petition for leave to appeal to the court of the United States having jurisdiction to review the merits of the underlying decision of the agency adversary adjudication. If the court denies the petition for leave to appeal, no appeal may be taken from the denial. If the court grants the petition, it may modify the determination only if it finds that the failure to make an award, or the calculation of the amount of the award, was an abuse of discretion.

EFFECT ON OTHER LAWS SEC. 206. Nothing in section 2412(d) of title 28, United States Code, as added by section 204(a) of this title, alters, modifies, repeals, invalidates, or supersedes any other provision of Federal law which authorizes an award of such fees and other expenses to any party other than the United States that prevails in any civil action brought by or against the United States.

LIMITATION 

SEC. 207. The payment of judgments, fees and other expenses in the same manner as the payment of final judgments as provided in this Act is effective only to the extent and in such amounts as are provided in advance in appropriations Acts. 

EFFECTIVE DATE AND APPLICATION SEC. 208. This title and the amendments made by this title shall take effect of October 1, 1981, and shall apply to any adversary adjudication, as defined in section 504(b)(1)(C) of title 5, United States Code, and any civil action or adversary adjudication described in section 2412 of title 28, United States Code, which is pending on, or commenced on or after, such date.

TITLE III-SMALL BUSINESS EXPORT EXPANSION ASSISTANCE SEC. 301. (a) The Secretary of Commerce, after consultation with the Administrator of the Small Business Administration, the Presi dent of the Export-Import Bank of the United States, the President of the Overseas Private Investment Corporation, and the Director of the Internal Revenue Service, shall establish an export promotion center in each of two district offices of the International Trade Administration of the Department of Commerce which are located in metropolitan areas where district offices of the Small Business Administration and the Internal Revenue Service exist. 

(b) The Export-Import Bank of the United States, the Internal 

Revenue Service, the Overseas Private Investment Corporation, the Department of Commerce, and the Small Business Administration shall each designate at least one full-time employee to serve as such agency's full-time representative in each such center. Each person designated shall be familiar with the needs and problems of small business exporting and shall serve without regard to the provisions of title 5, United States Code, governing appointments in the competitive service, and without regard to chapter 51, and subchapter III of chapter 53 of such title relating to classification and General Schedule pay rates. Each export promotion center shall serve as a one-stop

PUBLIC LAW 96-481–OCT. 21, 1980 

94 STAT. 2331 information center on Federal Government export assistance, financing programs available to small business, and other provisions of law governing exporting for small business. (c) Not later than six months after the enactment of this title, the Report to Secretary of Commerce shall report to the Select Committee on Small congressional 

Business and the Committee on Banking, Housing, and Urban Affairs committees. of the Senate and the Committee on Small Business and the Committee on Foreign Affairs of the House of Representatives on the progress made in implementing the provisions of this section. (d) Within two years after the date of enactment of this title, the Secretary of Commerce shall evaluate these export promotion centers, including, but not limited to, an analysis of the effectiveness of the centers in developing and expanding small business exports, and a comparison of the effectiveness of the centers in relation to district offices of the Department of Commerce which do not have an export promotion center. Such evaluation shall be submitted to the committees of the Senate and the House of Representatives referred to in subsection (c). (e) This section shall take effect on October 1, 1980, or on the date of the enactment of this section whichever occurs later and shall expire on October 1, 1983.

SEC. 302. (a) The Secretary of Commerce (hereinafter referred to as the "Secretary") is authorized to make grants (including contracts and cooperative agreements) to a qualified applicant to encourage the development and implementation of a small business international marketing program (hereinafter referred to as "the program"). Each qualified applicant under this title may receive a Federal grant not to exceed $150,000 annually for each of three years: Provided, That not more than one-third of these Federal funds may be used for the purpose of hiring personnel. Nothing in this section shall be construed as authorizing the Secretary to enter into contracts or incur obligations except to such extent and in such amounts as are provided in appropriation Acts.
