THE PURPOSE OF THE BILL 

The purpose of the bill, H.R. 6429, is to allow individuals and small businesses to be reimbursed for their court costs and associated expenses if they are successful in an action with the Government, unless it is shown that the Government was substantially justified in its claim or that special circumstances would make such an award unjust. This legislation is intended to respond to a chronic problem small business owners have had contesting or challenging the unreasonable exercise of Government authority because of the time and expense required to challenge the vast resources of the Federal Government. It is expected this bill will cause agencies to be more deliberative in their regulatory activity.

BACKGROUND AND INTRODUCTION 

The committee's Subcommittee on SBA and SBIC Authority and

General Small Business Problems held 3 days of hearings on this bill, April 17, 23 and May 1. During these hearings testimony was

received in support of this bill from individual small business owners who have encountered difficulties with the Federal Government, trade associations, and other private and Government witnesses. 

Throughout the hearings the committee was provided with numerous examples of Government actions against individual small business owners that were indefensible. Many witnesses stated that they had been frustrated by the maze of the agency review processes. All too frequently, the review process of an agency was unknown to the private citizen. Consistently, the committee was told of agency insensitivity to the problems of the small business community. Without exception, small business owners indicated this legislation would be beneficial to them and would enable many of them to better defend themselves and fight unwarranted Government charges that previously have gone unchallenged.

Based on the hearings, a number of refining amendments were considered and accepted by the subcommittee during markup. Significant discussions occurred concerning the eligibility for award of fees in the case of a settlement, a withdrawal of the case by the Government and a partial decision for a private party. Additionally, the eligibility of an intervenor for reimbursement and the standards to be used to determine the Government liability were carefully considered. 

The definition of a prevailing party posed a difficult problem. The subcommittee members, in response to testimony, deleted the word "prevailing" from the statute in order to clearly indicate that a final decision by an agency or the court against the Government was not an essential requirement for the award of costs and fees associated with the dispute. The subcommittee believed that in addition to those private parties who prevail on all issues in litigation or by final administrative order, a party also should be eligible for recovery if he obtains a favorable settlement, a voluntary dismissal, or where he may be deemed to have prevailed due to a decision in his favor or prevailed on less than all the issues or if the amount of the judgment against his was only a fraction of the amount the Government sought. 

The subcommittee also added the phrase "direct and personal interest" to the definition of "eligible party for costs and fees" to insure that only a party that has been or will be injured can benefit under the statute. The subcommittee determined to specifically exclude intervenors.

The subcommittee also carefully considered the alternative standards for award of fees proposed by the Department of Justice. It rejected the Justice Department's recommendation that the burden of proof be shifted to the individual. The subcommittee also agreed not to accept the Justice Department's proposal that the standard for award be changed from "not substantially justified" to a more rigid "arbitrary or capricious" standard.

Lastly, the subcommittee removed the provision of the bill requiring that the private party show, upon judicial review, that the decision by an agency was an abuse of discretion. The subcommittee believed that the presumption of regularity should not be attached to the agency's decision to award fees and costs.

The bill, with amendments, was reported unanimously from the subcommittee on May 6, 1980. The full committee again considered whether it would be appropriate to reduce the standard necessary for

the Government to justify its action from "substantially justified" to "arbitrary, frivolous, unreasonable or groundless." An amendment to alter the standard was soundly defeated. The full committee ordered the bill favorably reported on May 13, 1980 by a vote of 29 to 0, without any additional amendments.

NEED FOR THE LEGISLATION 

During the last two decades there has been an unprecedented expansion of Government regulation of small business. This, in turn, has spawned a dramatic increase in the number of administrative and judicial proceedings brought against small businesses by Federal agencies. Due to small business' limited resources and the Federal 

Government's inexhaustible assets, an obvious inequality in dispute resolutions has surfaced. This combination, coupled with the effect of legal principles that do not recognize these changes, has created a case resolution system that, for a small business, is based almost exclusively on economic criteria rather than what is right. The result is an unbalanced regulatory enforcement system that emphasizes, virtually to the exclusion of all other issues, the cost of potential litigation.

As is often the case, legal principles developed in England prior to the 18th century still remain in force in the United States. The "American Rule" which requires that each party shall bear his/her own cost of litigation in such a principle. The purpose of the rule was to insure that parties would assert or protect their rights without fear of being penalized for their actions. It did not and does not recognize the disparity of resources between the opposing parties. The administration of justice was believed to be better served if all parties know that the only costs one had to bear were his own. ()f course, such a rule is based on the assumption that the costs of securing or vindicating one's rights has an equal economic impact on each party to a dispute. The rule only makes sense when each party is equally affected by the costs of litigation. 

When an individual or small business is required to defend against an unreasonable Federal action, the disparity in the resources each brings to the dispute is enormous. This strikes at the essence of the rule which requires each to bear the burden of the costs independently. 

Testimony before your committee made this point very clear. Small business owners, if confronted with a regulatory dispute with the Government, routinely pay fines and/or sign consent orders, however unjustified or unreasonable. Similarly put, regardless of the merits of the situation, it is cheaper for the businessman to give in than it is to fight the Federal agency. This type of action, based solely on the cost of fighting back, seriously undercuts our system of justice. 

In cases involving Government action, small business does not have the resources to defend itself against unjustified claims. Further, the cost of vindication routinely exceed the amounts at stake. Lastly, the 

Government does not have the economic incentive or disincentive to closely evaluate the reasonableness of its case before proceeding. For these reasons it appears that the American rule is inappropriate in actions involving the Government and individuals or small businesses. 

The result, of course, is a Federal enforcement system that operates

unfettered by the usual checks and balances that are common to our system of Government. The existence of this policy can only lead to a further erosion of the belief that this is a Government of laws and not of men.

This is not to say that all Federal actions are unjustified or unreasonable. Unfortunately, the expansion of quasi-judicial regulatory mechanisms has significantly increased the number of Federal actions that clearly do not meet the usual standards of fairness expected of this Government. The impact of an unreasonable action nonetheless carries the full power of the Federal Establishment behind it and is legitimatized by that power. Therefore, when faced with that overwhelming power, the small business owner capitulates even though that action is improper.

The testimony of a number of small businesses before the committee can be summarized as follows: "My lawyer told me he thought I had a good chance to win, but the costs would be so great it would be cheaper to pay the fine." Certainly, there can be no greater frustration for a small business already struggling to comply with numerous Federal regulations, than to find out that it is not enough to be right. To continue to allow the financial situation of the small business or individual to determine whether an unjust action is contested, is clearly unreasonable.

The costs of contesting a Federal action quickly mount up. In addition to attorneys' fees, a specialist in that area of Federal regulation often must be hired to provide advice that is even partially equal to that of the Government's 67,500 attorneys. The cost of responding to Government discovery, and the accumulations and summarization of the business files must also be counted. Experts are often necessary, as are special studies. All these costs must be added to the time the business owner spends away from the day-to-day operation of the business where he or she usually represents the entire financial and operational talent of the business. For example, one witness testified that he spent $40,000 in attorneys' fees alone, defending against one unreasonable Federal suit of 4 years duration and that suit was dismissed by the court when it finally came to trial. The $40,000 represented 2 years' earnings (not profits) for this small firm of 16 employees.

It must be noted that the real aim of this legislation is not to spend great sums to pay the costs of fighting unwarranted Federal action. 

Rather, it is intended that the act will force the Federal departments and agencies to substantially improve the quality of their enforcement and other proceedings. But let there be no mistake, your committee clearly intends that where a small business or individual prevails against the Government either in toto or in substance, reasonable costs and fees shall be paid to him unless the Government can show that its action was substantially justified. 

WHAT THE BILL WOULD DO TITLE I 

Title I would require the Chief Council for Advocacy of the Small Business Administration to cooperate and consult with all appropriate

Federal agencies in an effort to maximize equal access to justice for small businessmen and women. 

The Chief Counsel is further required to collect the necessary data to evaluate the usefulness of this act toward facilitating the redress of injury caused by unreasonable Government action. A report is required to be submitted to the President and Congress which would insure that information is available to assess whether the objectives of the legislation would have been achieved. This report would be issued prior to the 3-year statutory termination of this act. 

TITLE II 

Title II seeks to eliminate the barriers that prohibit small businesses and individuals from securing vindication of their rights in civil actions and administrative proceedings brought by or against the Federal Government. The act would accomplish this by permitting small businesses and individuals to recover attorneys' fees and other costs of litigation from the Federal Government. Your committee recognizes the need to balance the rights of small businesses and individuals who seek review on the merits and the need for vigorous and appropriate enforcement of our laws. Therefore, the act carefully defines the test for determining the appropriateness of such an award, the parties which are eligible for an award and the instances when such an award can be considered.

WHO CAN CLAIM 

To be eligible for an award of attorney fees and other costs, the bill would require a party to be either an individual whose net worth does not exceed $1 million, or a business entity or other organization whose net worth does not exceed $5 million. The definition of "party" would exclude the Government, as well as those who do not have a direct and personal interest in the action. It is the belief of your committee that this act should not provide funds for intervenors, friends of the court, or others who have not been injured or likely to suffer irreparable harm.

WHEN A CLAIM CAN BE MADE 

The bill would require that in order to be eligible for an award of costs and fees, either a plaintiff or defendant must have "prevailed." 

Substantial discussion on the definition of "prevailing" led your committee to remove the word from the bill in order to more clearly indicate that a final decision on the merits is not the only instance where an award may be made. The committee intends that awards shall not be limited to a favorable decision by an administrative law judge, agency, department or multidepartment review board or court. It expects that an award may be made where a settlement or a dismissal, either voluntary or directed, has occurred in favor of a private party or where the private party has prevailed on less than all the issues. 

A party could also be deemed to have prevailed for purposes of fee award prior to a final judgment. For example, fees may be awarded on the basis of an interim order which is central to the case or where an interlocutory appeal is sufficiently significant or discrete to be

treated as a separate unit. Your committee intends that the occasions where an award may be made shall be broadly construed. 

WHEN A CLAIM CAN BE DENIED 

Your committee intends that costs and fees shall be awarded to an eligible private party in a civil dispute when the private party has prevailed, unless the Government can show that its action was substantially justified or that special circumstances make an award unjust. 

This standard balances the constitutional obligation of the executive branch to faithfully execute the laws with the public's interest in the vindication of private rights.

The test of whether a Government action is substantially justified would be essentially one of reasonableness. Where the Government can show that its case had a reasonable basis, both in law and in fact, no award would be made. In this regard, the strong deterrents to contesting Government action that currently exists require that the burden of proof rest with the Government. This allocation of the burden, in fact, reflects a general tendency to place the burden of proof on the party who has easier access to and knowledge of the facts in question. 

Your committee believes that it is far easier for the Government, which has control of the evidence, to prove the reasonableness of its action that for a private party to marshal the facts to prove that the 

Government was unreasonable.

Your committee specifically rejected the standard found in the 

Christianburg Garment Co. v. Equal Employment Opportunity Com mission, 434 U.S. 412, 421 (1978). The committee believes that the proposal to shift the burden of proof to the private party as required by the Christianburg standard was clearly too restrictive and contrary to the intent of the bill. The implication in the Christianburg case that plaintiffs be preferred over defendants or that either plaintiff or defendant be favored, one over the other, was considered inappropriate. 

Any attempt to add restrictive tests was not considered acceptable. 

The substantially justified standard would require that the court review the Government's action, without presumption, on the basis of the record. The Government would be required to show that its actions were substantially justified by the law and the facts if it is to avoid paying these fees.

Certain types of case dispositions may indicate that Government action was not substantially justified. A court should look closely at individual cases, for example, where there has been a judgment on the pleadings, where there is a directed verdict or where a prior suit on the same claim has been dismissed or lost on the merits. Such cases clearly raise the possibility that the Government was unreasonable in pursuing the action or was merely forum shopping. 

The standard, however, should not be read to raise a presumption that the Government position was not substantially justified simply because it lost the case, likewise, no presumption of reasonableness should be attached to the Government's actions. Nor would the standard require the Government to establish that its decision to litigate was based on more than a reasonable possibility of prevailing. Furthermore, the Government should not be held liable where "special cir-

cumstances would make an award unjust." This "safety valve" will help to insure that the Government is not deterred from advancing in good faith a close question of law or fact. It also would give the court discretion to deny awards where equitable considerations dictate an award should not be made, such as when there is unwarranted delay by the private party.

WHAT CAN BE CLAIMED 

Your committee expects awards to include the reasonable costs incurred in adequately preparing for and prosecuting a case. This includes, but is not limited to, the reasonable fees for attorneys and expert witnesses, and the cost of reports, studies or tests reasonably needed to support the private party's case. In addition, the award should include the cost of the party's personal absence from business at an hourly rate. With respect to attorneys' fees, they would be based on the prevailing market rate for the kind and quality of services rendered. The act specifically would provide that no attorney may be compensated at a rate in excess of $80 per hour, unless special factors justify a higher fee.

SOURCE OF FUNDS FOR CLAIMS 

In addition, this bill would stipulate that all funds for the awarding of fees must come from the budgets of the agencies, or departments whose actions have been adjudged to be not substantially justified. 

This fiscal responsibility is intended to make the agencies and departments more accountable for their actions. The impact of awards from the agency budgets would provide a quantitative measure of agency abuses and would encourage congressional oversight of departmental and agency practices and regulations. 

CONCLUSION 

The small business community has been denied its due process and equal protection rights guaranteed by the Constitution due to the cost of disputing the Federal Government. In far too many cases, a small business is forced to accept an unjust settlement or ruling simply because it does not have the financial ability to request a further review of the matter on the merits. On the other hand, your committee received testimony from small business owners who nonetheless elected to fight the Government on principle and ultimately won but then faced bankruptcy due to the costs involved. They may have won the battle, but have lost the war to survive.

Your committee believes that those small businesses who elect to fight a decision by the Government should be able to recover their costs. It is clear that the reimbursement of costs will not make them whole, but cost reimbursement from the agencies' budget will act as a deterrent to those agencies and departments which unfairly use their economic might to force unjust settlements and decisions upon those who do not have the resources to fight back. 

It should also be noted that authorizing reimbursement for attorney fees and other costs was 12th among the recommendations of the re cently concluded White House Conference on Small Business. Your

committee fully agrees with this priority and urges its endorsement by the entire membership of the House.

With regard to subdivision (B), relating to the statement required by section 308 (a) of the Congressional Budget Act of 1974, the following statement is made relative to the legislation: 

"Section 3(a) (2) of the Congressional Budget Act defines the term 'tax expenditures' as those revenue losses attributable to provisions of the Federal tax laws which allow a special exclusion, exemption, or deduction from gross income or which provide a special credit, a preferential rate of tax, or a deferral of tax liability."

None of the provisions of H.R. 6429 deals with taxation and thus, in your committee's opinion, this bill does not provide new or increased tax expenditures.

Section 3 (a) (2) of the Congressional Budget Act defines the term 

"budget authority" as authority provided by law to enter into obligations which will result in immediate or future outlays involving Government funds.

Under this definition, it is the final action of Congress that authorizes an agency to enter into obligations which constitutes new budget authority. In those cases which entail (1) an authorization for an agency to undertake a program; (2) an authorization for appropriation of funds to permit that agency to enter into obligations; and (3) an actual appropriation of such funds, it would be only the final action of Congress in appropriating funds which constitutes the budget authority.

None of the provisions of H.R. 6429 appropriates funds and thus, in your committee's opinion, the bill does not provide new budget authority. Accordingly, no comparison of budget authority, outlays or tax expenditures or 5-year projections have been made.

CONGRESSIONAL BUDGET OFFICE COST ESTIMATE 1. Bill number: H.R. 6429. 

2. Title title: Small Business Equal Access to Justice Act. 

3. Bill status: As introduced and referred jointly to the House Committee on the Judiciary and the Committee on Small Business, February 5, 1980. 

4. Bill purpose: The purpose of this legislation is to provide for an award of attorney fees, expert witness fees, and other costs to certain parties who prevail in a review of, or in defending against, government actions in certain administrative adjudications and civil actions. 

The prevailing party is entitled to these awards only if the government does not show that its action was substantially justified. The legislation expires three years after enactment. 

5. Cost estimate: 

Estimated costs

Fiscal years (In millions of dollars): 1981 1982 1983 1984 1985 $153 187 228 

6. Basis of estimate: The following presents the methodology used to estimate the cost of this legislation. For the purpose of this estimate. an effective date of October 1, 1980 has been assumed.

Case loads 

A key determinant of the potential costs of this legislation is the total number of cases affected by the bill. These cases would be those that the government would lose and for which it could not or would not show substantial justification for prosecuting. As mandated by the bill, tort and Tax Court cases have been excluded. The bill also excludes from coverage individuals having a net worth of $1 million or more, and any sole owner of an unincorporated business, or any partnerships, corporations, associations, or organizations having a net

worth of $5 million or more. No estimate is available of the proportion of cases so excluded. Also excluded were those cases for which fees and other costs are already authorized, such as Freedom of Information Act and Title VII cases. In fiscal year 1979, approximately 45,000 cases involving the federal government terminated in the district, claims, customs, and appeals courts (4,083 tax cases and 40,880 other types of civil cases). It was assumed that the government would lose 15 percent of the tax cases and 20 percent of all other cases. These assumptions are based on a Justice Department survey which indicated that in fiscal years 1976 and 1977, the government lost an average of 15 to 25 percent of its cases. It was then assumed that the government could not or would not attempt to show a substantial justification for prosecuting in 25 percent of the cases lost. (In some cases, the cost in both time and dollars of such a justification may result in a Justice Department decision not to contest the awarding of fees, even when substantial justification may exist.) Therefore, the base number of cases for which an award of attorney fees and other costs is made was assumed to be approximately 2,200 cases per year, or less than 5 percent of the relevant judicial proceedings involving the government. Case loads have been increasing in recent years at a rate of approximately 15 percent per year. This rate of increase has been applied to the 1979 base case load in projecting the number of cases affected by the bill. 

The number of administrative adjudications for which awards would be made was based on preliminatry estimates for 1978 from the 

Administrative Conference of the United States. Approximately 240,- 

000 cases were terminated in 1978 after review by an administrative law judge. The number of administrative adjudications that the government loses is estimated to be 45 percent of the total, larged based on Social Security Administration cases which account for about 90 percent of the total and experience a loss rate of between 40 and 50 percent. 

Employing the same award rate (25 percent) as was used for judicial proceedings, it was estimated that awards would have been made in approximately 31,050 administrative cases in 1979, again increased by 

15 percent per year over the projection period.

Awards 

The estimated average award for judicial proceedings, excluding tax cases, was based on two surveys of awards in cases involving Title VII 

actions, Freedom of Information and Privacy Act litigations in 1977. 

The average award in these cases was approximately $16,000 for attorney fees. This amount was assumed to apply to all of the judicial cases estimated above, except tax cases. In addition to attorney fees, it was assumed that an award would be made for other costs incurred, such as expert witness, fees, the cost of the party's absence from work, costs of studies or reports, and any other costs deemed necessary for the support of the case. This additional amount was assumed to be $2,200, increased each year by the CBO projection of the consumer price index (CPI). 

Average awards in tax cases were estimated to be $10,000 for cases in the District Court, and $25,000 in the Court of Claims and the Appeals Court, also increased by the projection of the CPI. The total cost for all judicial proceedings was therefore estimated to be approximately $54 million in fiscal year 1981, increasing to $74 million in fiscal year 1983.

The awards for administrative adjudications for all types of cases were estimated to be $2,000 on average for attorney's fees and $200 for other costs. Other costs were assumed to increase annually by the CBO projection of the increase in the CPI. The total cost for administrative adjudications was estimated to be $92 million in fiscal year 1981, increasing to $124 million in fiscal year 1983, based on historical caseload levels.

Additional impact of bill 

The costs derived above were increased by 5 percent in 1981, 10 percent in 1982, and 15 percent in 1983, to reflect the reporting requirements specified in the bill and the impact that enactment of this bill would have on the size of awards and the number of cases. This was done for two reasons. First, since the government must show substantial justification for its actions, more time and money is expected to be spent by the government and private attorneys in establishing whether an award will be made. Second, since the bill states that "it is the purpose of this Act to diminish the deterrent effect of seeking review of, or defending against, governmental action. case loads in both judicial and administrative proceeds are expected to increase faster than the historical rate by virtue of the existence of this legislation. 

7. Estimate comparison: None. 

8. Previous CBO estimate: On July 18, 1979, CBO prepared a cost estimate for S. 265, a similar bill ordered reported by the Senate Committee on the Judiciary. This estimate differs from the previous one primarily because it is based on more recent and more complete data on the number of administrative adjudications, resulting in a substantially higher estimate of awards for that purpose. 

9. Estimate prepared by: Kathy Weiss. 

10. Estimated approved by:

C. G. NUCKOLS, For James L. Blum, 

Assistant Director for Budget Ananlysis.

In compliance with clause 7, rule XIII of the House of Representatives, the committee makes the following statement:

The costs attributable to this bill for the current fiscal year and for fiscal years 1981-85 are as follows:

Your committee does not quarrel with the $594 million cost estimate supplied by the Congressional Budget Office, but it appears that the CBO avoided the deterrent to unreasonable actions the committee believes will occur. The figures supplied by the Congressional Budget Office assume that the Federal Government will continue to bring unjustified actions against small businesses and individuals instead of being more selective in the actions it brings. If on the other hand the current

pattern continues, it is only right that the Government pay the cost of wrongfully bringing legal actions. Finally, your committee stresses that such payments will not require the appropriation of additional funds because any such payments are directed to be absorbed by the offending department or agency. 

Your committee has not received a cost estimate on H.R. 

6429 from any Government agency but we were advised that the estimate of the Congressional Budget Office reflects input from the Department of Justice.

In your committee's opinion, the above statements fully comply with the Rules of the House of Representatives. 

SECTION-BY-SECTION ANALYSIS TITLE I-SMALL BUSINESS ADMINISTRATION, OFFICE OF ADVOCACY Section 101 amends title II of Public Law 94-305 by adding new subsection (11) to section 202 and by adding a new section 207 and reunmbering the remaining sections. 

New subsection (11) requires the Office of Advocacy of the Small Business Administration to advise, cooperate with, and consult with the Attorney General of the United States, Federal agencies, and the 

Chairman of the Administrative Conference to facilitate relief af forded under this act.

New section 207 requires the Chief Counsel for Advocacy to submit a report to the President and to Congress every 2 years on (1) the number, character, and dollar value of cost and fee awards paid pursuant to this act; (2) the time period between the initiation of the formal action upon which costs and fees were awarded to a small business and the award and payment of costs and fees to the small business; (3) the number of instances where a Federal agency appealed the award of costs and fees under this act; and, (4) an assessment of whether this act has caused expeditious resolution of small business disputes with Federal agencies.

TITLE II-SMALL BUSINESS EQUAL ACCESS TO JUSTICE 

Section 201 contains a statement of the congressional findings and the purposes of the bill.

Subsection (a) finds that certain individuals and organizations may be deterred from seeking review of, or defending against, unreasonable governmental action because of the expense involved. 

Subsection (b) finds that because of the resources and expertise of the Federal Government, the standard for awarding fees should be different than the one applied in nongovernmental litigation. 

Subsection (c) states that the purpose of the act is to diminish the deterrent effect of seeking review, or defending against, unreasonable governmental action by authorizing an award of attorney fees and other costs.

Subsection (d) states that an additional purpose is to insure an exception to the "American rule" regarding attorney fees in actions by or against the United States.

Section 202 of the bill amends chapter 5 of title 5, United States 

Code, by adding new section 504, to provide for an award of reasonable fees and expenses to prevailing parties in agency adjudications under section 554 of title 5, sections 204 or 401 of title 39, or section 605 of title 41. 

Under the proposed section 504(a) of title 5, a party with a direct and personal interest other than the United States who prevails in an agency adjudication is entitled to reasonable fees and other expenses unless the agency finds that the position of the agency as a party to the proceedings was susbtantially justified or that special circumstances make an award unjust. The agency may reduce the amount to be awarded, or deny an award, to the extent that the private party during the course of the proceedings engaged in conduct which unduly and unreasonably protracted the resolution of the matter in controversy. 

It also defines terms used in the section. 

Under the proposed section 504(b), "fees and other expenses" include the reasonable fees of attorneys and expert witnesses, the cost of any report, study, or test which is found by the agency to be reasonably necessary to the party's case, as well as the cost of the party's personal absence from business at an hourly rate. The amount of fees awarded shall be based on the prevailing market rates for the kind and quality of the services furnished except that attorney fees are limited to a maximum of $80 per hour unless special factors justify a higher fee. "Party" means those individuals who are defined as "persons" under section 551 of title 5 who have a personal interest in the case, except it excludes those individuals whose net worth exceeded $1 million and it also excludes sole owners of unincorporated businesses, partnerships, corporations, and associations, including cooperative associations as defined in the Agricultural Marketing Act (42 U.S.C. 1141j (a)) whose net worth exceeded $5 million at the time the adjudication was initiated. "Adjudication" excludes any adjudication for the purpose of establishing or fixing a rate or for the purpose of granting or renewing a license.

The proposed section 504 (b) (2) incorporates by reference the de finitions of section 551 of title 5. 

The proposed section 504 (c) (1) directs each agency in cooperation with the Chairperson of the Administrative Conference to establish rules governing the procedure for awarding fees. It further specifies that where the underlying adjudication is reviewed by a court, an award shall be made under 28 U.S.C. 2412(d) (3), a new section added by this act.

The proposed section 504 (c) (2) provides that a party who is dissatisfied with an agency decision regarding attorney's fees may petition for leave to appeal to the U.S. court which would have jurisdiction to review the merits of the underlying decision. Review by the court is discretionary and a denial of a petition for leave to appeal is not reviewable. If a petition is granted, the court may reverse or modify the fee determination if it finds that the agency's determination was not substantially justified based upon a de novo review of the record.

The proposed section 504 (d) provides that awards will be paid out of the budget of the agency involved and also specifies that no additional funds may be appropriated to cover the costs of fee awards. 

The proposed section 504 (e) directs the Administrative Conference of the United States to report annually regarding the number and amount of fee awards under this section. 

Section 202(b) of the bill amends the table of contents to reflect the addition of section 504.

Section 202 (c) of the bill repeals section 504 of title 5 at the end of 3 years.

AWARD OF ATTORNEY FEES AND OTHER EXPENSES IN CERTAIN JUDICIAL PROCEEDINGS Section 203 (a) of the bill amends section 2412 of title 28, United 

States Code, by adding new provisions regarding the award of attorney fees and other expenses in certain judicial proceedings. 

The proposed section 2412(a) preserves the law of existing section 2412 which provides for a discretionary award of costs in any civil action brought by or against the United States except attorney fees and related costs.

The proposed section 2412 (b) permits a court in its discretion to award attorney fees and other expenses to private parties in civil litigation involving the United States to the same extent it may award fees in cases involving private parties. 

The proposed section 2412 (c) (1) incorporates language found in existing section 2412 which establishes that judgments for costs are to be paid as provided for in sections 2414 and 2517 of title 28. 

The proposed section 2412 (c) (2) establishes that fees and other expenses under subsection (b) are to be paid in the same manner as costs unless the award is based upon a finding that the United States acted in bad faith. In those circumstances, the award is to be paid by the governmental entity found to have acted in bad faith. 

The proposed section 2412(d) authorizes an award of reasonable fees and other expenses in judicial proceedings analogous to the awards authorized in agency adjudications under section 202 of this act. 

The proposed section 2412(d) (1) provides that a private party in certain civil actions brought by or against the United States is entitled to reasonable fees and other expenses unless the Government action was substantially justified or other circumstances make an award unjust. The subsection applies to all civil actions except tort actions and those already covered by existing fee-shifting statutes. 

The proposed section 2412(d) (2) defines terms which apply to this subsection. The definitions of "fees and other expenses" and "party" are explained under section 202 (a) of this act. 

The proposed section 2412 (d) (3) provides that a court may award fees and other expenses to a private party upon judicial review of an agency adjudication, and shall include in the award the fees and other expenses for services performed during the administrative proceedings unless the court finds that during such proceedings the

19. position of the United States was substantially justified or that special circumstances make an award unjust. 

The proposed section 2412(d) (4) established that awards under this subsection will be paid by the agency involved and specifies that no additional funds may be appropriated to cover the costs of fee awards. 

The proposed section 2412(d)(5) directs the Administrative Office of the U.S. Courts to report annually regarding the number and amount of fee awards under this subsection. 

Section 203 (b) of the bill conforms the item relating to section 2412 in the table of sections for chapter 161 of title 28. 

Section 203 (c) of the bill repeals subsection (d) of section 2412 of title 28 at the end of 3 years.

Section 204 (a) of the bill deletes subdivision (f) of rule 37 of the 

Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. 

Section 204 (b) of the bill conforms the table of contents of the 

Federal Rules of Civil Procedure. 

Section 204 (c) deletes the existing language regarding awards of fees under title 12, United States Code, section 1988. 

Section 205 of the bill provides that nothing contained herein shall affect any other provision of law which authorizes the awarding of fees to any party other than the United States. 

Section 206 of the bill provides that these amendments shall apply to any adjudication or civil action pending on the effective date or commenced after the effective date of this act. 

Section 207 requires the Office of the Chairman of the Administra tive Conference of the United States and the Director of the Administrative Office of the U.S. Courts to provide to the Small Business 

Administration's Office of Advocacy the information required to be collected under title I of this bill. 

Section 208 provides that the effective date of this act is October 1, 1980.

TITLE 5, UNITED STATES CODE 

PART I-THE AGENCIES GENERALLY 

SEC. 

CHAPTER 5—ADMINISTRATIVE PROCEDURE SUBCHAPTER I-GENERAL PROVISIONS 

500. Administrative practice; general provisions. 

501. Advertising practice; restrictions. 

502. Administrative practice; Reserves and National Guardsmen. 

503. Witness fees and allowances. 

504. Costs and fees of parties. 

$504. Costs and fees of parties (a) An agency, or the United States Postal Service, that conducts an adjudication subject to section 554 of this title, sections 204 or 401 of title 39, or section 605 of title 41 shall award, to a party with a direct and personal interest other than the United States, fees and other expenses incurred by that party in connection with that proceeding, unless the adjudicative officer of the agency finds that the position of the agency as a party to the proceedings was substantially justified or that special circumstances make an award unjust. The adjudicative officer of the agency may reduce the amount to be awarded, or deny an award, to the extent that the private party during the course of the proceedings engaged in conduct which unduly and unreasonably protracted the final resolution of the matter in controversy. The decision of the agency under this section shall be made a part of the record and shall include written findings and conclusions and the reason or basis therefor. (b) (1) For the purposes of this section— (A) "fees and other expenses" includes the reasonable expenses of expert witnesses, the cost of any study, analysis, engineering report, test, or project which is found by the adjudicative officer of the agency to be reasonably necessary for the preparation of the party's case, the cost of the party's personal absence from business at an hourly rate, reasonable attorney or agent fees and other reasonable and necessary costs directly related to the party's case. The amount of fees awarded under this section shall be based upon prevailing market rates for the kind and quality of the services furnished, except that (i) no expert shall be compensated at a rate in excess of the highest rate or compensation for experts paid by the agency involved; and (ii) attorney fees shall not be awarded in excess of $80 per hour unless the agency determines by regulation that an increase in the cost of living or special factors, such as the limited availability of qualified attorneys for the proceedings involved, justifies a higher fee;

(B) "party" means a "person" as defined in section 551 of this title, who has a personal interest in the case, but excludes any individual whose net worth exceeded $1,000,000 at the time the adjudication was initiated, and any sole owner of an unincorporated business, or any partnership, corporation, association, including a cooperative association as defined in the Agricultural Marketing Act (12 U.S.C. 1141j(a)) or organization whose net worth exceeded $5,000,000 at the time the adjudication was initiated; (C) "adjudication subject to section 554 of this title, section 

204 of title 39, or section 601 of title 41" does not include adjudication for the purposes of establishing or fixing a rate or for the purposes of granting or renewing a license; and (D) adjudicative officer means the official of the agency, whether designated as an administrative law judge or hearing officer or otherwise, who presided at the adjudication. (2) Except as otherwise provided in paragraph (1), the definitions provided in section 551 of this title apply to this section. 

(c) (1) After consultation with the Chairman of the Administrative 

Conference, each agency shall by rule establish uniform procedures for the submission and consideration of applications for an award of fees and other expenses. If a court reviews the underlying decision of the agency adjudication, an award for fees and other expenses may be made only pursuant to section 2412(d) (3) of title 28. (2) A party dissatisfied with the fee determination by the agency may petition for leave to appeal to the court of the United States having jurisdiction to review the merits of the underlying decision of the agency adjudication. If the court denies the petition for leave to appeal, no appeal may be taken from the denial. If the court grants the petition, it may modify the determination of the agency if it finds that the failure to make an award, or the calculation of the amount of the award, was not substantially justified based upon a de novo review of the record. (d) Fees and other expenses awarded under this section shall be paid by the particular agency from any sums appropriated to such agency, except that no sums may be appropriated to any such agency specifically for the purpose of paying fees and other expenses awarded under this section. 

(e) The Office of the Chairman of the Administrative Conference of the United States shall report annually to the Congress on the amount of fees and other expenses awarded during the preceding fiscal year pursuant to this section. The report shall describe the number, nature, and amount of the awards, the claims involved in the controversy and any other relevant information which may aid the Congress in evaluating the scope and impact of such awards. Each agency shall provide the Conference with such information as is necessary for the Chairman to comply with the requirements of this subsection.

§ 2412. Costs 

[Except as otherwise specifically provided by statute, a judgment for costs, as enumerated in section 1920 of this title but not including the fees and expenses of attorneys may be awarded to the prevailing party in any civil action brought by or against the United States or any agency or official of the United States acting in his official capacity, in any court having jurisdiction of such action. A judgment for costs when taxed against the Government shall, in an amount established by statute or court rule or order, be limited to reimbursing in whole or in part the prevailing party for the costs incurred by him in the litigation. Payment of a judgment for costs shall be as provided in section 2414 and section 2517 of this title for the payment of judgments against the United States.] 

§ 2412. Costs and fees 

(a) Except as otherwise specifically provided by statute, a judgment for costs, as enumerated in section 1920 of this title, but not including fees and other expenses of attorneys, may be awarded to the other party in any civil action brought by or against the United States or any agency or department, including the United States Postal Service, or official of the United States acting in his official capacity in any court having jurisdiction of such action. A judgment for costs when taxed against the Government shall, in an amount established by statute, court rule, or order, be limited to reimbursing in whole or in part the other party for the costs incurred by him in the litigation. (b) In addition to the costs which may be awarded pursuant to subsection (a) and unless otherwise specifically prohibited, a court may award reasonable attorney fees to the private party in any civil action brought by or against the United States or any agency, department or official of the United States acting in his official capacity, or the United States Postal Service, and the United States shall be liable to

the same extent that a private party would be liable under the common law or under the terms of any statute which specifically provided for such an award. (c) (1) Any judgment against the United States or any agency or official of the United States acting in his official capacity for fees and other expenses pursuant to subsection (b) shall be paid as provided in sections 2414 and 2517 of this title and shall be in addition to the compensation, if any, awarded in the judgment. (2) Any judgment against the United States or any agency or official of the United States acting in his official capacity for fees and other expenses pursuant to subsection (b) shall be paid as provided in sections 2414 and 2517 of this title unless the basis for the award is a finding that the United States acted in bad faith, in which case the award shall be paid by the agency found to have acted in bad faith and shall be in addition to the compensation, if any, awarded in the judgment. (d) (1) In addition to the costs which may be awarded pursuant to subsection (a) and except as otherwise specifically provided by statute, a court shall award fees and other expenses to any party other than the United States which in any civil action (other than cases sounding in tort) brought by or against the United States in any court having jurisdiction of that action, unless the court finds that the position of the United States was substantially justified or that special circumstances make an award unjust. The court, in its discretion, may reduce the amount to be awarded pursuant to this subsection, or deny an award, to the extent that the private party, during the course of the proceedings engaged in conduct which unduly and unreasonably protracted the final resolution of the matter in controversy. A party seeking an award of fees and other expenses shall, within thirty days of final judgment in the action, submit to the court an application which provides evidence of such party's eligibility for the award and the amount sought, including an itemized statement from attorneys and experts stating the actual time expended in representing such party and the rate at which fees were computed. 

(2) For the purposes of this subsection— 

(A) "fees and other expenses" includes the reasonable expenses of expert witnesses, the cost of any study, analysis, engineering report, test, or project which is found by the court to be reasonably necessary for the preparation of the party's case, the cost of the party's personal absence from business at an hourly rate, reasonable attorney fees and other reasonable and necessary costs related to the party's case. The amount of fees awarded under this subsection shall be based upon prevailing market rates for the kind and quality of the services furnished, except that (i) no expert shall be compensated at a rate in excess of the highest rate of compensation for experts paid by the United States; and (ii) attorney fees shall not be awarded in excess of $80 per hour unless the court determines that an increase in the cost of living or special factors, such as the limited availability of qualified attorneys for the proceedings involved, justifies a higher fee; (B) "party" means any individual whose net worth is less than $1,000,000 at the time the civil action was filed, any sole owner of

an unincorporated business, or any partnership, corporation, association, including a cooperative association as defined in the Agri cultural Marketing Act (12 U.S.C. 1141j(a)) or organization whose net worth was less than $5,000,000 at the time the civil action was filed; and (C) "United States" includes any agency, and any official of the United States acting in his official capacity. (3) In awarding fees and other expenses under this subsection to a private party in any action for judicial review of an agency adjudication conducted pursuant to section 554 of title 5, the court shall include in the award, the fees and other expenses for services performed during the administrative proceedings unless the court finds that during such proceedings the position of the United States was substantially justified, or that special circumstances make an award unjust. (4) Fees and other expenses awarded under this subsection shall be paid by the particular agency from any sums appropriated to such agency, except that no sums may be appropriated to any such agency specifically for the purpose of paying fees and other expenses awarded under this section. 

(5) The Director of the Administrative Office of the United States 

Courts shall include in its annual report prepared pursuant to section 604 of this title, the amount of fees and other expenses awarded during the preceding fiscal year pursuant to this subsection. The report shall describe the number, nature, and amount of the awards, the claims involved in the controversy and any other relevant information which may aid the Congress in evaluating the scope and impact of such awards.

SECTION 722 OF THE REVISED STATUTES 

SEC. 722. The jurisdiction in civil and criminal matters conferred on the district and circuit courts by the provisions of this Title, and of 

Title "CIVIL RIGHTS," and of Title "CRIMES," for the protection of all persons in the United States in their rights, and for their vindication, shall be exercised and enforced in conformity with the laws of the United States, so far as such laws are suitable to carry the same into effect; but in all cases where they are not adapted to the object, or are deficient in the provisions necessary to furnish suitable remedies and punish offenses against law, the common law, as modified and changed by the constitution and statutes of the State wherein the court having jurisdiction of such civil or criminal cause is held, so far as the same is not inconsistent with the Constitution and laws of the United States, shall be extended to and govern the said courts in the trial and disposition of the cause, and, if it is of a criminal nature, in the infliction of punishment on the party found guilty. In any action or proceeding to enforce a provision of sections 1977, 1978, 1979, 1980, and 1981 of the Revised Statutes, title IX of Public Law 92-318, [or in any civil action or proceeding, by or on behalf of the United States of America, to enforce, or charging a violation of, a provision of the United States

ADDITIONAL VIEWS OF HON. JOHN J. LAFALCE 

As one of the cosponsors of the Small Business Equal Access to Justice Act, I wish to voice my support for the principles embodied in H.R. 6429. Title II of this bill will provide needed relief to small business concerns and individuals who successfully resist unreasonable government action by reimbursing them for their expenses including fees. This provision is virtually identical to a measure which I introduced in the last Congress (H.R. 11224). Such reform is long overdue and it is clear to me that the time is right for the Congress to provide the necessary relief for those who are lacking the necessary resources to effectively resist improper governmental conduct.

The concept of this bill is something that has been endorsed by the Administration and by the Department of Justice. However, the particular wording of the bill has been vigorously opposed by the Department of Justice.

I, too, am concerned about several provisions in the bill which I believe will be unduly burdensome and which will likely promote both uncertainty and an additional layer of litigation. My principal concern is that the bill's standard for awarding attorneys fees requires an agency (or court where appropriate) to award expenses unless it finds that the government's position was "substantially justified or that special circumstances make an award unjust." I am concerned that this provision is too subjective and insufficiently precise to provide the necessary guidance to ensure consistent application by the courts and agencies. It invites the decision maker to either second guess or to apply his or her subjective judgment as to what is justified. Further. I am concerned, because of the lack of certainty, that this provision will be the subject of a significant amount of litigation as to its interpretation, and thereby, unduly prolong many law suits to the detriment of the parties and our system of justice. 

I also express concern over two other provisions, which in addition to the standard of proof, I do not believe were intended by the authors of this legislation when we started out. First, the bill mandates that a court (or agency) award a fec. I believe we should give the courts (or agencies) some discretion to deny a fee award. I would expect that once it has been determined that the government has violated the standard, the court (or agency) would ordinarily award reasonable fees. In unusual circumstances, however, it may be unjust to award fees. Although it is not likely that a court (or agency) would often deny a fee award, it ought to be provided with the discretion to do so.

Second, the bill places the burden on the government to indicate its own conduct of lawsuits, thereby creating a questionable, and possibly dangerous, presumption that the government's decision to bring a lawsuit was unjustified in suits it loses. In carrying its burden of persuading the judge that its decision was substntially justified, the government, in each case it lost, would be required to come forward with evidence to defeat an award of attorney fees. It is unsound public policy and legal procedure to impose this burden absent a finding of misconduct.

A presumption arises in the law either because of the probability that the presumed fact is true when the facts on which it is based are established, or because of the difficulties in proving the presumed fact. The presumption that the position of the government was not substantially justified when it loses, is not based on either of these factors. In fact, there has existed throughout our history a common law presumption that supports a contrary conclusion-public officials perform their duties lawfully and in good faith. This presumtion is an integral part of the body of case law governing Federal administrative and official actions. H.R. 6429 would have the effect of setting aside two centuries of practice and would overrule this common law presumption.

I think that it is imperative, if we are to authorize the relief this bill seeks to provide, that we do so in as concise and precise a fashion as possible. By doing so we will ensure that the agencies, courts and litigants will clearly understand what we intend to accomplish. Further, I believe we must be careful to avoid any measure that will itself breed additional litigation.

Accordingly, I proposed amendments to H.R. 6429 to avoid the problems noted above and yet, still accomplish our objective of providing the wherewithal to resist unreasonable government conduct. 

Each of the amendments had the strong backing of the Department of Justice. The first amendment would have brought H.R. 6429 more in line with the Department's own bill, H.R. 7208. However, that amendment was defeated by the Small Business Committee. Next, instead of offering two other amendments which represented narrower versions of my first amendment, I offered an amendment which represented, I was advised, the irreducible minimum that would be required in order to obtain the approval of the Department of Justice. 

I proposed to change the standard of proof required by the government from "substantially justified" to the standard enunciated by the Supreme Court, and as interpreted and applied on numerous occasions by the courts with little or no dispute as to its meaning: unless the government shall prove that its action was "neither arbitrary, frivolous, unreasonable, or groundless."

This language is taken from the Supreme Court in the case of Christiansburg Garment Co. v. E.E.O.C., 424 U.S. 412 (1978) which established a standard for certain types of fee awards in Civil Rights cases when the action of the opposing party was "frivolous, unreasonable, or groundless." It seems to me that the purpose of H.R. 6429 is to deter unreasonable government conduct. Indeed, that is precisely what the bill provides in section 201 (c) (1). The standard I proposed will accomplish everything that H.R. 6429 is intended to accomplish and will avoid uncertainty, inconsistent application, and an excessive amount of litigation concerning its interpretation. However, this amendment was also defeated by the Small Business Committee. 

In conclusion, I am sure that my colleagues in the House will agree with me that our effort to provide needed relief to those who are unable to effectively resist unreasonable government conduct should not be impeded by a provision that carries with it significant adverse effects incident to its interpretation. 

In the past, by authorizing the award of attorney fees and other expenses in certain instances, we have encouraged private citizens to advance the interest of specific, important social goals. For example, we have authorized the award of attorney fees in cases brought pursuant to the Civil Rights Act, the Freedom of Information Act, and the Consumer Product Safety Act. H.R. 6429 represents a dramatic departure from our previous, categorical approach. Bearing this in mind, I believe that the Christiansburg Garment standard is much better suited to solving the problem H.R. 6429 seeks to remedy. 

I, therefore, urge my colleagues to carefully consider this issue to ensure that any bill we approve will accomplish the ends we are secking without creating unnecessary additional burdens for the agencies, courts and litigants. ויי

I also encourage my colleagues to consider an approach that will obtain the approval of the Department of Justice so as to avoid a veto of this bill. In striving for passage of this bill in its present form, we may well be ensuring that it never becomes enacted. 

JOHN J. LAFALCE.

Senate, 96th Cong., 1st Sess.; on S1444, a bill to amend the

FEB 1981 

Applicability of Equal Access to Justice Act to Tax Court Proceedings 

The Equal Access to Justice Act 1/ ("the Act") amended the Judicial Code to permit the award of attorney's fees and other expenses to prevailing parties in civil litigation involving the 

United States. 2/ The Act revised section 2412 which incorporates section 1920. These two sections have referred to court fees and costs, since the Judicial Code was revised and codified in 1948. The question presented is whether the United States Tax 

Court has the authority to make such awards under the Act. The position of the Internal Revenue Service, based on the Act, two widely-cited Tax Court opinions, and legislative history, is that the Act does not authorize the Tax Court to award costs, attorney's fees and other expenses.

The pertinent provisions of the Act are as follows: 

"S2412. Costs and fees "(a) Except as otherwise specifically provided by statute, a judgment for costs, as enumerated in section 1920 of this title, [28] but not including the fees and expenses of attorneys, may be awarded to the prevailing party in any civil action brought by or against the United States or any agency and any official of the United States acting in his or her official capacity in any court having jurisdiction of such action. A judgment for costs when taxed against the United States shall, in an amount established by statute, court rule, or order, be limited to reimbursing in whole or in part the prevailing party for the costs incurred by such party in the litigation. "(b) Unless expressly prohibited by statute a court may award reasonable fees and expenses of attorneys, in addition to the costs which may be awarded pursuant to subsection (a), to the prevailing party in any civil action brought 

1/ Title II of P.L. 96-481. 

2/ It also amended 5 U.S.C., not pertinent herein.

by or against the United States or any agency and any official of the United States acting in his or her official capacity in any court having jurisdiction of such action. The United States shall be liable for such fees and expenses to the same extent that any other party would be liable under the common law or under the terms of any statute which specifically provides for such an award. "(c) (1) Any judgment against the United

States or any agency and any official of the United States acting in his or her official capacity for costs pursuant to subsection (a) shall be paid as provided in sections 2414 and 2517 of this title and shall be in addition to any relief provided in the judgment. "(2) Any judgment against the United

States or any agency and any official of the United States acting in his or her official capacity for fees and expenses of attorneys pursuant to subsection (b) shall be paid as provided in sections 2414 and 2517 of this title, except that if the basis for the award is a finding that the United States acted in bad faith, then the award shall be paid by any agency found to have acted in bad faith and shall be in addition to any relief provided in the judgment. "(d) (1) (A) Except as otherwise specifically provided by statute, a court shall award to a prevailing party other than the United States fees and other expenses, in addition to any costs awarded pursuant to subsection (a), incurred by that party in any civil action (other than cases sounding in tort) brought by or against the

United States in any court having jurisdiction of that action, unless the court finds that the position of the United States was substantially justified or that special circumstances make an award unjust. (emphasis added)

"(4) (A) Fees and other expenses awarded under this subsection may be paid by any agency over which the party prevails from any funds made available to the agency, by appropriation or otherwise, for such purpose. If not paid by any agency, the fees and other expenses shall be paid in the same manner as the payment of final judgments is made in accordance with sections 2414 and 2517 of this title." (emphasis added). 

As stated in section 2412(a), a court may award those "costs" 

enumerated in section 1920 of Title 28. Section 1920 does not define the term "court" but simply refers to "any court of the 

United States." That term is defined in section 451 as follows: "As used in this title: 

The term "court of the United States" includes the Supreme Court of the United States, courts of appeals, district courts constituted by chapter 5 of this title, including the Court of Claims, the Court of Customs and Patent 

Appeals, the Customs Court and any court created by Act of Congress the judges of which are entitled to hold office during good behavior."

As passed by the House of Representatives in 1948, the revised Judicial Code would have included the Tax Court in all respects, thereby placing it within the provisions of Title 28 and removing the Court from the Internal Revenue Code. The House version of the bill specifically listed the Tax Court as a "court of the 

United States" in section 451. As finally enacted, all references to the Tax Court in the bill were deleted in order to avoid any controversy in moving the bill to enactment. S. Rep. No. 1559, 80th Cong., 2d Sess. 2 (1948).

To date, the Tax Court remains governed by Title 26, the 

Internal Revenue Code. See sections 7441 through 7448. It is created under Article I of the U.S. Constitution I.R.C. section 7441. Its judges hold office for a term of 15 years. 

I.R.C. section 7443 (e). "Courts of the United States" are created under Article III of the Constitution, judges of which are entitled to hold office during good behavior. See 28 U.S.C. section 451.

Prior to 1969 the Tax Court was an independent agency of the

Executive Branch. See S. Rep. No. 91-552, 91st Cong., 1st Sess. (1969); 1969-3 C.B. 423, 615. Nothing in the 1969 legislation granted the Court any authority under the Judicial Code. 

In Sharon v. Commissioner, 66 T.C. 515 (1976) the taxpayers sought to invoke sections 2412 and 1920 of the Judicial Code to recover as "costs, their Tax Court petition filing fees. In holding that their reliance on those two sections was "misplaced," the Court stated "[t]hose sections apply only to courts 'the judges of which are entitled to hold office during good 

The United States Tax Court was consciously excluded from that definition of a court. S. 

Rept. No. 1559, to accompany H.R. 3214, 80th 

Cong., 2d Sess. 2 (1948)." id., 534. 3/

In Key Buick v. Commissioner, 68 T.C. 178 (1977) the Tax 

Court denied the taxpayer's motion for attorney's fees under the so-called Allen amendment to 42 U.S.C. § 1988 4/ which permitted district courts to award such fees in tax proceedings brought by the United States. In all Tax Court proceedings the action is brought by the taxpayer. Thus the Court concluded the statute had no applicability to the Tax Court. In addition, the Court noting that the statute provided for the award of fees "as part of the costs, concluded, "[t]his Court has no jurisdiction to allow costs to a taxpayer" id. 179, citing Sharon as authority. 5/ "1 As noted, the Act revised section 2412 which has been in the Judicial Code since 1948. New sections 2412 (b) which provides for attorney fees and 2412(d) which provides for "fees and other expenses" do not specifically refer to section 1920, which enumerates those costs which may be awarded. However, both sections 2412 (b) and (d) refer to fees and costs "in addition to" those "awarded pursuant to subsection (a)." Thus the statute as revised contemplates 

3/ Also see Olick v. Commissioner, 73 T.C. 479, 490 (1979),

Ellis v. Commissioner, 40 CCH Tax Ct. Mem. 267, 270 (1980), and 

Cox v. Commissioner, 40 CCH Tax Ct. Mem. 619, 625 (1980). 

4/ The Allen amendment was deleted by the Act. 

5/ On appeal, the Fifth Circuit affirmed the Tax Court's denial of the motion based on the identity of the moving party but in dictum cast doubt on the Tax Court's interpretation of its power to assess attorney fees "as cost. 613 F.2d 1306 (5th Cir. 1980).

an integrated basic scheme for the award of costs, attorney's fees and other expenses in "courts of the United States. This is reinforced by sections 2412(c) (1) and (2) and (d)(4) (A) of the Act which provide, respectively, that payment of judgments for costs pursuant to subsection (a), fees and expenses of attorneys pursuant to subsection (b), and fees and other expenses pursuant to subsection (d) are to be paid as provided in sections 

2414 and 2517 of the Judicial Code. The latter two sections refer to the district courts and the Court of Claims, respectively. 

Again, the Tax Court is not mentioned. Similarly, sections 

2412(a), (b) and (d) each refer to "any court having jurisdiction of such (that) action." It would be a strained reading of the 

Act to interpret this common language one way for purposes of section 2412 (a) and another for purposes of sections (b) and (d). 

Thus it would seem anomalous to suggest that attorney fees and other expenses could be awarded by the Tax Court on the authority of sections 2412 (b) and (d) if the Court has no authority to award costs under section (a). If the Tax Court is not a "court of the United States" for purposes of "costs" it would be similarly excluded for purposes of attorney's fees and other expenses.

In This conclusion is confirmed by the legislative history. the section-by-section analysis of the Act contained in H.R. Rep. 

No. 96-1418, 96th Cong., 2d Sess., 17 it is stated: "Section 2412 (b) permits a court in its discretion to award attorney fees and other expenses to prevailing parties in civil litigation involving the United States to the same extent it may award fees in cases involving other parties. The courts so empowered are those defined in section 451 of Title 28, United States 

Code. This is consistent with the present law in section 2412. (emphasis added)

In addition the Congressional Budget Office analyzed drafts of the Act on three occasions. In each CBO report, the following identical language appears: "[a]s mandated by the bill, tort and Tax Court cases have been excluded. 

S. Rep. No. 96-953, 96th Cong., 1st Sess. 10, also see p. 12;

H.R. Rep. No. 96-1005, Part 1, 2nd Sess. 13; and H. R. Rep. No. 

96-1418, supra 22. 

It would appear that Congressional leaders in the 96th

Congress were aware of the fact that the Equal Access to Justice Act would apply to adversary proceedings between citizens and the government including tax litigation in the U.S. district courts but that an amendment to the Internal Revenue Code would be required

to provide for attorney fees in the U.S. Tax Court. One of the sponsors of the Equal Access to Justice Act, Senator Nelson, also cosponsored S. 1444, the Taxpayer Protection and Reimbursement 

Act. The latter was not acted upon and died in the 96th Congress. 

When the Act was debated and passed by the Senate, Senator Nelson said: "Finally, Mr. President, I believe the Senate should again provide for the awarding of attorney's fees for small business in Tax Court cases. While this bill provides for the fees to be paid in tax-related matters being heard in the U.S. district courts, it is not available in cases in the Tax Court. I have joined Senator 

Baucus and others in cosponsoring S. 1444, a bill to amend the Internal Revenue Code to provide for the award of reasonable attorney fees. That legislation should similarly be given rapid consideration by the Congress. 

Cong. Record, July 31, 1979, pg. S. 10922.

S. 1444 would have amended the Internal Revenue Code to specifically provide for the award of "reasonable court costs, including attorney's fees" in "any civil action or proceeding in a court of the United States, including the Tax Court, Chief Judge of the Tax Court, Hon. C. Moxley Featherston testified before the Subcommittee on Oversight of the Internal 

Revenue Service of the Senate Finance Committee with regard thereto. The Court took no position on the basic policy of the proposed legislation but commented on some of the pertinent aspects of the bill. Referring to the provision for the award of "reasonable court costs" Judge Featherston's statement provides, "[w]e interpret this language to mean that 'court costs' other than attorney's fees are not limited to the precise items now allowable in the district courts under sections 1920 and 2412 of Title 28 of the Code. 1/"

Hearings Before the Subcommittee On Oversight of the Internal Revenue 

Service of the Committee on Finance 96th Cong., 1st Sess. 77 (1979) (statement of Chief Judge C. Moxley Featherston). The footnote, perhaps a reminder of the Tax Court's view of the applicability of section 1920 to its proceedings, states,

"[t]his section does not apply to the Tax Court because the meaning of the term 'court of the 

United States' is restricted by 28 U.S.C. 451 to courts created under Article III whereas the Tax Court was created under Article I of the 

Constitution." id.

Thus, it is concluded that the Equal Access to Justice Act does not apply to proceedings in the United States Tax Court. Steven | Mozinh
